LADIES' LETTER.

MY Dias Nzun. - There is a distinct change in the shape and trimming of hats this season, and the disposition of the trimmings is even more altered than the shape of the hats. Many smart hats are trimmed with folds of glac6 silk veiled with tulle, plain, spotted or beaded, but these need careful arrange- ment if they are to be neither too formal nor yet untidy-looking. A coarse blue straw hat, with folds of white silk covered lightly with silver- spotted blue tulle round the crown, four small white wings at the back and a cluster of corn- &wen' underneath, is useful yet stylish, and such a model might be worn with almost any kind of gown.

Ithough the shape described—that is to say, one that has a nearly flat brim much upturned at the back and which is worn well forward—is per- haps the one most generally popular at the moment, a newer style of hat is that which has a broad brim drooping in front and behind, but it is raised at the sides by thick wreaths of flowers or masses of bows or rosettes. It is a shape that looks its best trimmed with two or more long ostrich feathers starting from a buckle or handsome paste ornament in the centre of the front and sweeping round to the back, lying gracefully on the brim. This hat is often seen in white or cream chip, with feathers of the same colour, and pink roses or carnations mixed with rosettes of old Imo beneath the brim rt,t above the ears. A meet useful unlined blouse, which can be worn on warm days squally with a smart skirt or with a plain one of alpaca, can he made in alpaca, washing silk, or cambric. It is extremely pretty in silk and ecru insertion lace, and this being thin, should be made up on a very thin lining, which can he cut exactly like the silk, but laid fiat under the tucks, and thus preserving the loose effect.

Some delightful morning blouses can be made in cambric. They can be made most simple. Two or three tucks are stitched, then a line of insertion embroidery is sewn on, and three more tucks made, end the front can be shaped, and made up in the usual manner. There is only a plain left side wit.hont fulness, and the front fastens over this, the frill falling quite to the edge, and leaving the entire front in one piece. The sleeves are small, and of the shaped bishop style, finished with cuffs of insertion. This model is pretty in pink or blue linen, with white insertion, and can be worn with turn-down collar and cuffs.

Smart gowns for tine sunny days are now the chief desideratum, and if idle season is to last well into August, as predicted, gowns of the lightest texture should be required, if we are to make up for the long spell of unfavourable weather already experienced. The very soft, limp, woollen fabrics are in demand this season, as they hang in the ap- proved fashion, and also make up well into frills, pleatings, and bonillonnees. In the very soft materials, those of plain colour, such as nun's veiling, barege, cashmere, voile, and similar tex- tures, are mostly favoured, and trimmings are of the most ornate description. Narrow ribbon, used as frills, or rows in groups of the IJ4b6 width, are still very fashionable, and those appliqu•S garland laces are seen everywhere, and used alike on cloth or muslin. Frills are also fastened down at inter• vale with lace motifs, which can be cub from the garland trimmi LIZ%

dainty little gown can be made of light blue linen, mixed with blue pin-spotted cambric, the s_pot in a brighter and deeper blue. The deep flounce is gored, like an ordinary skirt, and, there is only a little fulness at the front and sides. The edge is simply hemmed up rather deeply, and stitched by machine, and the top of the skirt is made in yoke fashion, fitting closely, and the flounce joined on with a band ot linen covering the seem. The cambric yoke is gauged in lines, and the everpart of btue linen is put on fell, and out- lined with a narrow stitched band of linen. A black silk dress looks well with a plain and fairly full skirt, and a bodice with tight back and erinlitiingghtofbne amr raodwo slightly ponchesdilfkroenat.gedT!l:lit,blhOtialef by a beaded gimp, and a full with relavceerehoefad eki vest of black lace, or, if prcferred, of tucked silk, striped with rows of the gimp sewn between the tucks. Have a narrow folded belt of silk with a iet clasp,, and finish the wrists and neck with lace

Your loving JENNIE:.

"WOMEN'S CHAT."

Her Majesty has celebrated yet another jubilee—her jubilee as " Lady of Balmoral." The Queen purchased Balmoral in the summer of 1848, half a century ago, and took possession of it in the following September. To commemorate the event,* fountain is to be erected on a suitable spot, on the Ballater road. Although the ruler of the largest and richest empire tree world has ever seen, the Queen is by no Trlealls the richest person on the earth. Out of the £385.000 al- lowed her,all except £98,000 is spent by those responsi- ble for the maintenance of the Royal Household, and of the £96,000, £.38,C00 is put by for a rainy day, so that the exact amount received by the Sovereign is only £BO,OOO, as fixed by Parliament. 'I be Queen. however, possesses private means. The Duchy of Lancaster brings her £48,000 per annum,her land in Great Britain yields a rent roll of not less than £20,000 a year, and she owns also considerable property in Germany, sad New York. Claremont House, Osborne, and Balmoral too, are the private property of the Queen. The first named—the home of the Duchess of Albany—etanding in its estate of 480 acres, was bought for £74,000, a decided bargein,as it originally cost Lord Clive £150,000. Balmoral and Osborne are said to be worth about half a million sterl- ing. Norria Castle, concerning the purchase of which, by the Queen, there has been so much speculation of 13.te,is one of the most beautiful seats in the Isle of Wight. Sur- rounded by splendid treee,robed in ivy, and begirt with feir lawns, the Castle as seen from the Solent has a venerab:e appearance. As a matter of fact though, it enly dates from the beginning of the century, having been built for Lord Henry Seymour by Sir J.Wyattyille. George IV. was entertained there by Lord Henry in 1819 and subsequently the Queen when Princess Victoria, and 1 the Duchers of Kent, constantly resided at Norris. lo 1844 her lelejesty and Prince Albert were enxieue to buy I the Castlo (in preferenca to Osborne) but terms could not then be arranged. Like so many of the Queen's descendants, the young Princess Alice of Albany 13055,99 PS marked artistic lean- ings, and the patience and industry to apply herself toi the studies necessary to secure SUCCOSA in art. Her Roya Flift,hne3s is a great favourite with her eunt,the Princess Louise, perhaps the most gifted from an artistic point of view, of all the Queen's daughters; who is keenly Inter- Petted in the progress of her tierce. In all probability I Princess Alice will join the Royal circle, at one of next year's Drawing Rooms, Yonthfol Princesses of our Royal Family have always been treated as though their con- firmation was equiva;ent to the "coming nut" of less ex- alted members of society, and Princess Alice of Albany I Was duly included in a Queen's dinner party at Windsor. before the flight of the Court to the North. The celebrated vine at Hampton Court Palace, which with two or three exCeptione, is the largest in Europe. Is I showing signs of decay. It was planted in the year 1789, and has gone on bearing otter 2,000 hunches of grapes for the last hundred years. It now seems to be feeling the effeet r‘f old age, and this year much of the fruit is vine ,o,bf thealobni egf. belongsto et r itheas aa variety only nyi el y two wBo especiallyaot r Ham- burg,others s ie in removed and only about 1000 launcher; will be left to mature.,,nc3 .Tphoe, apart tt fliorrittabienCltle:E' can be compared with it. One is at Cumberland Lodge in Windsor Park,and the other is the famoue Breadalbene vine in Pertlaehire. Hampton Court Pelece is still erte of the finest of the royal palaces. The public are admitted free to the palace and gardens, but a emelt sum is charged for viewing the great vine. It is veonderfnl what strider, we women have made of late years, in the art of dining. Once upon a time, we were never suppesed to need mere than a few mersels- per day, and the woman who could appreciate and eat a good meal was regarded as nothing better than a savage. Now however we have changed all that, and without beeoming gourmets are able to enjoy and appreciate,with rine men folk, the good things of the table. Women's Clubs though, albeit of recent Innovation, seem scarcely to have greened the existing state of things, for the food one is offsred at these establishments is usually of the most indifferent kind. The tea and coffee is good enough, but the food is of the dullest and it never varies. Oily a week back, I beguiled a friend into a club, at which in its early days, I had partaken of a very excel- lent luncb,assnring bet the food was not in the least like that usually served at clubs devoted to our Use. But alas, the glory of the place hed departed, and my friend . looked reproachful as she strugglee with the tough and ' tastelese cutleter which lead been served to us. "My dear," she said at length, "the glass, silver, and able- arrangements generally, " are irrepreaehe ble I admit, but—a man's restaurant for me in future !" The origin of glass is uncertain, but it dates from hie) antiquity. The Egyptians. who made chemistry their study were arqueinted with it 5 000 or 6,000 years ago. The glass of Egypt was generally opaque, rarely trans- parent, and always colered. After tlie Egyptians, the People of antiquity most renowned for glass were the Phoeniciarta, who indeed were the legendary inventors of it. Early in the nineteenth century the Venetians, cultivated the art, and from them it extended late Ger. many and France. British table glass has not a very long history, nor bee it any merit to distinruittli it above that of other countries. Until late in the 1.66 h century; but little fine clese W*9 made, nor had it any chance of Competing with the b-autiful silver work which it was ultimately destined to supplant, as those who possess specimens of it must he well aware of, for whilst silver improves in appearance by being placed on white napery the lusterless badly-coloured old glass looks p< or and mean upon a table cloth. In the middle ages, the orna- mentation of the table was one of the greatest Pleasures of the Wealthy, and glass could not compete in size of the richness with the huge hanape, big spice plates. sil- ver harnessed horns, agate and capital cups, and nets, with which the rich surrounded themselves. Miss Dixon, who recently translated Tetrarch's letters is now in Tuscany, engaged in tracing the history of the woolen industry in that country. An excellent Italian scholar, Abe has alreedy done much, a fact WA cannot appreciate too highly, as it is only by studying the me- thods of these wise and cunning mediseval manufac hirers, that we can hope to improve our borne goods! For after all this nineteenth century has witnessed sad decadence in dress fabric. Beauty, durability, genuineness have all been sacrificed to the modern pas- sion for cheapness and show. The eollette,which is concocted out of a square, all the four points being utilised, is in high favour. These dainty additions to a toilette are built usually of chiffon, or silk, closely pleated in sun-ray pleating, edged all round with lace, and Mounted so es to allow one point , ack and for e t n the to fall en each shoulder, and the remaining points, One t fb7,nvett:, an d wh Embroideredite gauati ribbon is em- ployedll9ss figures inside the tares, ruff which finishes rff the neck. The shops at the present moment are inundated with the ready-made shirt and Nouse, but as ever their prettiness is usually of the catch-penny order. All hut the very expensive ores have that look, and even the best are most lamentably deficient in that subtle quality of cut, which can hardly be too diligently sought after, and too highly rated when found. By this means the most fascinating creations can be obtained at little cost; and the knowledge that in this respect at least, we are smartly turned out, will amply repay any trouble we may have tail( n. Because hot water is to be had for the making, many people think lightly of it, as a medical property. But in so doing they make a huge mistake. Nothing so promptly cuts short sore throat or rheumasism as hot water,whm applied promptly and thoroughly. Headache almost almost always yields to the simultaneous application of hot water to the feet and back of the neck. A strip of flannel folded lengthwise and dipped in hot water and wrung out, then applied r.,und the neck of a child that ' has croup will sometimes bring relief in ten minutes Hot water taken freely half an hour before bedtime is helpful in the case of constilation, while a glass of hot water taken just after rising, before breakfast, has cured thousands of indigestion, and no simpler remedy is more widely recommended by physicians to dyspeptics. Mackerel Pie.—Clean and slice two mackerel,add one bay leaf, six cloves, a sprig of parsley, sprinkle with pepper, and salt, add one teacupful of water, place in a dish, and cover with a crust. Bake half an hour in a moderate oven.

A WARRINGTON VOLUNTEER SEL‘GEA.NI'S FIAPPY

The 50:h anniversary of the incorporation of the borough of Warrington is an event of considerable re- pining on the part of its inhabitants, marking, as it does not only a considerable increase in its stz) and population but an enormous dovelopmsnt of its local industries and resources. But at No. 6, General-street, Warrington, resides a working man who i?, perhaps, at the present moment onu of the happiest and and /Wst thankful men U the town.

--- RYDER. His name is George Ryder. and be is 26 years of age. Ryder, who, by the way, is a volunteer sergeant much respected in the Warrington battalien, follows the trade i of a horse shunter on the railway, and about four years and a half ago he had occasion to rub down one of the horses under his care,which was suffering from scrofula. Shortly afterwards he noticed a" breaking-out "as he , termed it to a local reporter (who interviewed him and . gave him permission to publish his whole history), on . both of his hands. They commenced to itch, and in a short time his work, which had hitherto been a pleasnre to him,wa• carried on with considerable difficulty,while his rest was disturhe.d at night. One or two local medi- cal men were consulted, and their diagnosis was that be was suffering from eczema. Various remedies were tried, but none produced the deeired benefit. At length he wee compelled to obtain other advice, and for seven weeks was a patient in the Manchester and Salford Hospital. Benefit was obtained from had the treatment while he remained in hospital, but h ad no sooner returned to werk than the disease broke ont again with renewed vigour, and he found it necessary to go about with both hands, which were very much swollen, wrapped up and enclosed in cotton gloves. Occacioually the flesh was torn off his hands when he removed the bandages, and his condition was pitiable in the extreme. A Liverpool specialist was now consulted, but still the pais and trouble continued. It is a long lane,however, which has no turning. Life was becoming a burden to him,and he had almost begun to despair. All the money he could spare was spent in trying remedies recommended by friends and neigh- bours, but with the inevitable result—failure. At length one day, an eventful one in his history es it proved to be,a neighbour brought under his notice the ease of a woman who had been cured of a similar skin disease by the use of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pate People. An advertisement in a local newspaper relating to the pills was shown to him and he lost no time in ob. taming a box of the pills,andit was fortunate for him th it he did so. When the contents of the second box had been taken he noticed a great improvement in the condition of hie hands, and so satisfied was he with the treatment that a third, and then a fourth box were bought,and, to use his actual words, "I am better now than I have been all along." He no longer has occasion to go to work with covered hands, and he informed the reporter thafte regards Dr. Williams as a public ben factor, and blesses the day he heard of his pills. Ho eleepa well at night, is in excellent bodily health and no ono could tell from an examination of his hands that he ha] So long suffered from such a troublesome disease. He has been warmly congratulated by his friends and neigh- bours upon hie recoverY,and informed the reporter who interviewed him in the presence of several friends that be has infinite if the faith in the remedy, and believes that f the trouble should recur it can easily be removed by a continuance of the pills. An indication of the excellent s:ate of Ryder's beal:h is afforded by the fact that when the reporter Waite,l upon him at his house, on his return from work the °thee evening he was preparing his sccountreinents for a week's encampment with the local volunte.ir battelion at Rhyl, where, he said, he had no doubt he should not only be able to put in as much drill as any other man hut, freed from pain in his hands, he was happy as he had ever been in his life. Ryder is anxious that other people suffering from the same diseaee should be made aevainted with the won- derful effects of Dr. Williams' Piolt Pills. He was loud in his praises of their efficacious effects, about which in fact lie never seemed to tire of talking. He courts the fullest inquiry, and will be pleased to substantiate every word contained in this interview to any one who would like to call upon him. That, Ryder, who is highly reF- pected, careful, sober and industrious man, is iutensely thankful was evident from his conversation. He has found a cure f..r hie ailment, and he is anxious that . other sufferers should known of its existence,aed receive equal beLefic.

rim EXAMINER, F_RIDA Y, JUNE 24, 1898

"CAMAL" CHAT.

Art circles here are very deeply stirred by the destb of Sir l. Burne-Jones, and his mecits,persanal and professional, are the subjects of can ,ersatioa on all sides. His death adds the loss of anther great artist to those losses which the country so recently suffered by the death of Llrd Leighton and Sir John Millais. The obituary notices of him abound with anecdotes which show the lovableness of the man personally. It is remarkable the, he was rarely seen in the haunts and among the festivities of men. The same withdrawal from the gaze of public curiosity has been a characteristic of most of the greatest men of the century. One need only name Tennyson. Carlyle, Herbert Spencer, Darwin Ruskin, and Swinburne.

There are more reasons than the Welsh coal strike for the abandonment of the naval manceavres this year. The announcement has caused some con- sternation in naval circles, not to speak of the dock- yards. It is not merely a sufficiency of Welsh coal, but also the price, that is entering into the calcu- latione of the Admiralty; but it can hardly be that these would be sufficient reasons in a time of perfect international repose. The political element has as much to do with the abandonment of the mance•lyres land probably more than the stocks and price 01 coal, Britaiu needs to be strongly represented,both at the Philippines and in Chinese waters,and in the event of trouble arising there trouble will arise in Europe as well. But the resolute feeling v 7 ,ich has grown up in America to submit to nn dictation or inter- ference from Germany in reference to the Philip- pines appears to have had the eff set already of sobering the tone or German opinion and the aims of the German Government, and the catesmen of the Fatherland cannot conceal from themselves the fact that their country might citue out of a quarrel with America a much smaller "sea Power" than it at present is.

The town of Kano, ab)ut which so signal a blun- der has ben made in the Niger Convention, as is indicated by its surrender to France, is tile greatest town in the Central Soudan. It is (le most even- ' sive market in Central Africa. Its wails are 15 mile , in circnit.and its population is stated by those who I have visited it at 150,000. It is a commercial Mecca. to which flock all the traders ot Central Africa. Through he pass the Mahornmedan pilgrims who are on their way to the religieus Mecca in Arabia, and there they provision themselves for a long stage in their journey. It is not merely a market, it is a great manufacturing place. In it are woven the costliest cloths of the Soudan, and these it both wears and exports in enormous quantities. Indigo is largely cultivated around it,and dyeing is carried on to a great extent. Morocco leather is the work of Kano artisans, and most of the water skins used in the deserts of North Africa come from Kann. Sometimes a single caravan will 'convey £lOO,OOO worth of kola nuts from the west of the Niger,and from Kano they radiate as a luxury or a dissipa- tion along the . innumerable trade routes of which this town is the centre. One traveller estimates its annual trade at £15.000,000 sterling. The blunder is a very curious WI% and by it. probably hangs a strange tale ot bitter and protracted wgotiatious.

Lord Stanley signs the cheques, assists in directing the operations in the lobbies and dining-rooms as well es in the strangers' bars ; but unless Sir Wilfrid can find a means of bringing the question before a new court, or a cliff -rent magistrate. his position is exactly where it was. His case was not lost beca use he had Proceeded against the wrong person, It was lost ispQn its merits, and it was stronger against the barman than it can be against Lord Stanley.

A curious rumour has got afloat in Unionist quer- tots. to the effect that Mr. Chamberlain is contem- plating a personal move of a sensational kind. The versions given of his supposed intentions are vague and rather csntradictory, but they agree in the sup- position that he contemplates an early retirement frbm his present post. A favourite suggestion is that of a possible Rosebery•Chamberlain entente, but this may be dismissed offhand. Another notion, which has at least the stamp of greater probability, is that the Colonial Secretary may resign in order to make himself the leader of the large number of Unionists who are discontented with the foreign policy of the Goverement. That he is greatly dis- satisfied with his present position seems beyond all doubt, and if things take the turn which he appears to expect in the Far East and in West Africa his friends believe he will not remain to share in the shipwreck of a policy which he has never pre- tended to approve. Whatever may be the facts,the disquieting idea that he contemplates some sen- sational change has certainly got abroad in Con- servative circles, and the extent to which it is credited goes a long way to show how very. little in spite of all that has happened,the Colonial Secretary can count on Tory confidence.

It was Dirraeli who predicted that the battle of the Reformation would have to be fresght ov sr again in the Church of England. Meanwhile, the House of Commons is conducting some preliminary skir- mishes. Sir William Harcourt has thrown himself into the fray with an ardour that reminds one of old times. Not since the Agricultural Rating Bill was being crammed down the throat of Parliament has the leader of the Opposition shown so much persistency in criticism, or such unflagging devo- tion to the post of duty. Hour after hour on Tuesday night he stuck to his place, raising his voice every now and then to persuade.to warn,or to fulminate in echoing periods. His activity put new life into the scene. The dubious music of the division bell kept the lobbies alive throughout the night. An alert Opposition watched with lynx eyes every move in the Ministerial game The Government on their part had to tread warily lest their feet should he caught in the dangerous net of Ritualism. Their cue was to evade the issue so perseveringly raised by their opponents. "We agree with much that - you say." was Mr. Balfour's unvarying reply to the , Liberal leader," but we fail to see how the question arises here."

THE WEST ROOM: AN UNSOLVED MYSTERY.

By LUCY HARDY, Author of "That Terrible Christmas Eve," "A

Story of a Curse," dm.

We canoot select our own time for enduring attacks of the gout; or I should have thought it particularly inconsiderate of Frank's old uncle to have chosen the period ho did for being seized with what he calls "a visit from my old enemy," and summoning my husband to travel to the remote wilds of Scotland in order to receive "what may be my last words." We had just looked forward to such a happy time of rest and quiet after our wanderings abroad ; and, in present circumstances, Frank was particularly unwilling to leave me alone. We had been married just seven years; seven years of such happiness as falls to the lot of but few wedded folks, I think ; and the three children, at least, had thriven during their wandering life with their father's regiment. But I had had quite enough of "foreign service," and greatly rejoiced at Frank's appointment to a" depot" in a Western English county, where we had secured a charming old house, within an easy drive of the town, with a garden which charmed the hearts of the children. Just, however, as we had settled in our new home Callle an urgent letter from old Sir John, or, rather, from his doctor. The Scotch estate was entailed, and Sir John, a very grave and dignified old gentleman, was always fond of expatiating upon "the responsibilities which will hereafter devolve upon Francis, my direct heir, and his son John, my great-nephew." Somehow I never recognised dear old Frank and little Jack under these imposing titles. Whenever Sir John had an extra bad fit of gout he always—if Frank cfianced to be in Eng- land—expected him to rush off to Scotland to receive his farewells and testamentary instructions; and when my husband once reached Inverloch, it was difficult for him to escape again. Still, the old man had always been kind ui us after his fashion, and was solitary and ill; so I could not oppose my husband's departure; though I believed his uncle's "last attack " would pass off as many others had done, and this alarm for his life prove unfounded.

"I don't like leaving you, little woman," said my husband, "but if the poor fellow really is as bad as they say, it seems cruel to refuse to go to hint. I tell you what, I shall write and ask Rhoda to stay here while lam away—l can't leave you with only the servants." said, rather doubtfully. Truth to tell, I was not a little afraid of Rhoda, my husband's half-sister, whom I lcoked upon as one of those admirable pereOltflot destined to go through life rather respected than beloved. I knew Frank was greatly attached to her. His own mother had died in giving him birth, and his father had remarried within a year or two. Rhoda's mother (contrary to the established tradition) had proved a most kind and devoted step-parent; and Frank had always regarded the baby girl, born a few years later than himself, as his actual sister. The two had grown up together as inseparable companions, no other children arriving in the family. We had been so much abroad throughout our married life that I had seen very little of any of my husband's relatives, but I knew that Frank's marriage had not been exactly a source of satisfaction to any of them. Ills father was dead when our wedding took place, and Sir John eipressed no opinion Upon the matter; but I was fully aware that most of Frank's kindred considered that Captain Seton, young and handsome, and with the Scotch estates in prospect, might have "done better" than marry the penniless orphan of a country vicar. I had a very vivid recollection of a three days' visit to Frank's step-mother shortly after our marriage, during which, though I was treated with the most punctilious kindness and courtesy, I pelt that both Mrs. Seton and her daughter were weighing me in the balance—and finding me wanting. Of Rhoda, 6. keen-eyed, dark-haired, clever woman of twenty- five, I felt especial awe; and as conversation revealed toy deficiencies, my ignorance of the many "ologies " which Rhoda had at her fingers' ends, my apathy regarding the "burning questions " of the day, in which my sister-in-law was so deeply interested, I felt myself but a silly child, for all my twenty years and newly-acquired matronly dignity. I suspected that I was a disappointment to Frank's relatives; and Rhoda, though she received me kindly for my husband's sake, was too candid by nature to be able entirely to hide this feeling. Though we had always corresponded in srthodox relationly fashion, I had never mit my sister-in-law since that time, and the prosp(c; of having to entertain this—to me—rather formidable connection without Frank at hand to support me was not cheering. "You'll find out what a brick Rhoda is when von come, to know her Letter, Nell," said my husband. "I know she is rather stiff at first." "She is,,, I said fervently. "But she is the kindest and best-hearted girl in the world," went on Frank; and I murmured the acquiescence so often paid to the dictum that a repellent manner covers the "best heart in the world."

"But the children ? " I said doubtfully. "Oh, they'll get on all right; Rhoda adores children. Look here, little woman, I can't go away and leave you—with what is to happen soon —without some responsible person to look after you and the babies. Now, if Rhoda is here, I shall know everything is going on well, and that Jack won't be left to fall into the pond, or May to tumble out of the window, if you should be laid up while lam away. You won't mind having Rhoda here, will you, darling, to ease my mind? " Of course, I consented ; and when another imperative telegram called Frank away to Scotland that very afternoon, I was not, after all, sorry that my sister-in-law was to arrive the next day; for, formidable as I felt her to be, it was but a dreary thing to be left alone in the house with the s,rvants—and the new baby's arrival expected every day. Rhoda, at thirty-two, was but little altered from my memories of her seven 3 ears ago; the same rather abrupt manner, keen glances, and short, decisive speeches. But she surprised me when the children were introduced. "Are those the babies? —oh, you darlings! " she cried, opening her arms with an entirely new expression upon her face; and one of my fears—that the children would be afraid of her--was set at rest for ever..

• Indeed, it required but a day or two to make Rhoda the idol of the nursery; the children being utterly unawed by her talents, her Girton degree, her keen dark eyes. I actually once, on entering the nursery unexpectedly, found its lawful guardian absent, and my grave sister-in-law seated on the ground, with all her hair loose, while Jack mimicked the regimental barber, to the screaming delight of his two sisters ; Rhoda laughing as loudly as the children. She sprang up when I entered, and seemed half-vexed at having been thus detected; but my awe of her was considerably diminished from that day; and when Jack confi- dentially inforivel me that "Auntie Rhody was the jolliest am to in the world—she could tell better stories than O'Flynn," I knew his heart at least was won ; for O'Flynn (a disreputable soldier servant whose many delinquencies we had long winked at for the sake of his devotion to the children) was Jack's ideal of human excellence. To myself Rhoda was extremely kind and attentive, perhaps considering the children reflected some credit upon me; and I could truthfully write and tell Frank that I was "very glad " to have her with me, in the circumstances. For Sir John was still very ill, and, though the doctor was hopeful of his recovery, the old man himself piteously entreated Frank to remain with him ; and it seemed cruel to refuse. •,••

_ "Did you choose your own rooms when you came into this house, Ellen? " said my sister-in-law one day.

"No; we have taken the house furnished, and we occupy the Same bedrooms the last tenants did. Why do you ask ? " "Because I have been thinking that, when baby comes, you would be so far more comfortable in that large bedroom a the end of the passage than where you are now, opposite the nursery, where every little noise must disturb you." "I never thought about that; I always like to bo.near the children," I said.

_ "Let me occupy your present room," urged Rhoda. "Really, Ellen, you will be better further off. The children will be obliged to keep un- naturally quiet." "I hate keeping quiet," remarked Jack sotto rose, to which May responded, with all an elder sister's dignity, "You nover do." "I wish you would look at that West Room, went on Rhoda; "it is really the largest and the pleasantest bedroom in the house, and it is odd that the other tenants did not use it; but perhaps, like yourself, they wished to be near the nursery." I yielded to Rhoda's importunities, inspected the room, and was certainly struck with the size and

commodiousness or rue aisused apartment, witn its convenient and spacious dressing-room opening out of it. The house was one of those old-fashioned mansions to which successive architects have added rooms in all manner of unexpected corners. It was far larger than its present owners requsred, and they had left several of the apartments un- furnished, which afforded the children some lelightful playgrounds on wet days. This disused bedroom—the 'West Room," as it was designated on the label below its bell—was the largest in the house, but it had apparently been little occupied, though it was well furnished—in the somewhat Dumbrous style of several generations back. It was, in a way, cut off from the rest of the house; this apartment, and a small turret room above it, approached by a winding staircase, being "built DIA "at the end of a passage, and shut off from the other rooms by a stout oaken door. A spacious dressing-room opened out of the bedroom, and these two apartments and the little chamber above had apparently been added to the original house by a kind of afterthought on the part of some architect. . . . _

"When you and your nurse want to be quiet, how convenient this outer door will bet remarked Rhoda; and I agreed to have the room cleaned, aired, and made ready for my time of trial. Certainly, when I first took possession of the 'Vest Room one autumn evening, I could not help 'inking that my sister-in-law had been wise in her ...vgestion. I was feeling very nervous and ..pressed. and sometimes even the voices of the romping bairn ies across the way were a trial. Now I could sleep undisturbed, and without interfering with the children's play; and I had overheard some whispered plan between Jack and May for an invasion of "Aunty Rhody's room" at daybreak, and of the expected delights of a "bolster match" with that lady. "And you're better here, and they're better without you, ma'am," said my nurse, a pleasant, kindly woman, "for in the other room you'd be fidgeting every time Master Jack tumbled down; and how ever could I get the blessed new baby to sleep with those other little dears always about the door ? " Yes, this exchange of rooms had been wise, no doubt; and I drew my chair up to the fire, which was pleasant on an October evening, and, dismiss- ing my attendant, sat lingering awhile, basking in its genial glow before retiring to bed. Suddenly a feeling which I can hardly define seized upon me—a choking sensation of horror, of dread, as though I were beholding some crime which I was powerless to prevent; it is impossible to put the experience into words, but, with a convulsive shudder, I sprang from my seat and rushed to the opposite end of the room, as though to escape from —nothing! The cheerful firelight flickered and ehewing the eminently commonplace and com- fortable interior, as unlike the typical "haunted zoom " of tradition as could well be imagined; lind there was neither sight, nor sound, nor move- vent in the apartment; and yet—and yet—in toms way I felt an irresistible disinclination to return to my comfortable seat by the fire. I know not why it should be so, but most people appear to consider bed as a sanctuary from super- natural annoyances—"he drew the bedclothes over his head" is the orthodox description of the con- duct of a beholder of a night apparition. Though I had neither seen nor heard aught that could be described as "ghoNtly," I lost no time in shrouding myself in the sheets, and was speedily asleep, in spite of my previous tremours. The bright morning sunshine and the day's cares and occupations soon effaced the previous evening's disagreeable impression from my mind. Later in the day, not feeling as well as usual, I yielded to my nurse's advice to remain in my room and partake of an invalid meal by my own fireside instead of joining Rhoda at our seven o'clock dinner.

I was sitting, resting quietly after my repast, wondering if Frank would bo detained much longer in Scotland, and shedding a few tears as 1 thought of his absence just...now, when again there came upon me the same shuddering horror—the same sick sensation of nameless terror, and again I started up as one who flies from the spectacle of some crime. This time, however, 1 succeeded in controlling myself. "I am out of sorts and nervous," I said to myself, with resolute rebuke of my own folly, and I took up an engrossing novel which I had laid down a short time before, deter- mined to banish my fancies by its perusal. Sud- denly, in the empty apartnient overhead, came the sound of trampling footsteps moving rapidly and heavily about over the floor. "I must tell Nurse Harris to keep that door locked," I thought to myself, "for now the children have once found their way there they will be disturbing us constantly." . _ .

Heavily, very heavily for the footsteps of little ones, did the trampling steps fall above; then came the sound of a fall.

I sprang up; in doing so, I twisted my foot, and for a moment could not move. As the mother of three healthy, riotous children, I was pretty well accustomed to the sound of falls in the nursery, and as no accompanying roar was heard, I felt tolerably satisfied that no great harm had ensued from the tumble overhead. Jack was a philo- sophical little soul in such accidents, which befel him incessantly, and usually picked himself up, rubbed his bruises, and remarked: "Tumble down, tumble up, no bones broke! " But now the door overhead was cautiously opened, and a dragging, bumping sound as of something heavy being dragged down the stairs was heard—then another fall—just outside my door. "Children, what are you doing?" I cried, flying to open it, now really alarmed. No light outside —no children—all still as the grave. "Come down; why are you hiding? Is anyone Stud ? " I cried impatiently; and receiving no ..;eply, I dashed up the staircase. The room door above was locked -OM the outside—the key in the lock; I hastily turned it, opening the door; within was an empty, dusty apartment, dimly lighted by the moonbeams. I do not know how I regained my own bedchamber; I must, in some manner, have tottered back there, for the next thing I recol- lect was finding myself on my bed, with Rhoda and the nurse in anxious attendance, and the former saying that "the heat of the fire must have made me faint." I felt too ill and weak to talk much then; but through the wakeful hours of that weary night, during which my careful nurse never left me, I came to one fixed resolve. Never would I occupy that dreadful apartment another evening— never should my baby open its innocent eyes in such a terrible locale. That some unknown, un- detected crime had assuredly taken place in this part of the house I felt firmly convinced. Any- way, 1 was resolved to shift my quarters as early as possible. I dreaded Rhoda's opposition, but the greater fear cast out the lesser. As I had expected, my decision was energetically combatted by my sister-in-law. She pointed out in words of wisdom and reason, how "utterly absurd " it was to yield to fancies; how I had fallen into a half- faint from the heat of the fire, and, in this con- dition, had a kind of nightmare experience; how I should bring myself into a yet worse state of AOrves by weakly yielding to imaginary terrors, and light shift my room every day in obedience to ne fresh "fancy." Even the nurse, impressed by the commodiousness of the apartment I wished to forsake, joined in Rhoda's remonstrances; pointing out the advantages of the room for a sick chamber, and volubly assuring me "ladies so often had dreams and fancies at these times; she had nursed a lady," &c. "I will go to the workhouse infirmary rather than remain here! " I cried at length, with a passionate flood of tears; reckless now, for I felt I had lost the last shred of Rhoda's esteem, and would be for ever in her eyes a foolish, whimsical, altogether irrational creature. "Of ourse, if you are going to excite your- self in this manner, Ellen, there is no more to be said about the matter! " was Rhoda's rather cold reply. So I carried my point, and was tren,ferred to my old apartment Rhoda assisting in the work of removal with silent but very palpable displeasure; Nurse Harris was less reti- cent, and openly muttered about "ladies who gave themselves up to fancies." An unexpected ally, however, came to my aid when the old doctor 'looked in to see how I was going on," which he now did every few days. He found us busied in transferring my belongings from one room to the other; and Rhoda and Nurse Ilarris explained, in rather injured tones, that I :lad "taken a sudden dislike to the West Room, ~,saa would move out of it."

he West Room—do yon mean that shut-off the end of the passage ? I thought that was ...ler used now," said Dr. Willis rather sharply. "You know something shout that room," I said, for his tone was peculiar. "My sister-in-law was delightoo. with the room •at first," said Rhoda, "but she had a bad dream there one night, and took a Macy." "Dr. Willis, I 21m sure you know of some story about that room," I persisted, watching his face narrowly. "My dear young lady," he said lightly, "I ant sorry I cannot supply you with a proper ghost %tory, but I am afraid this house is neither old nor

aristocratic enougo to UvatiL a leanly spectre. You must take some baronial hall or historic mansion if you want that kind of thing. But I think you are quite as well out of that West Boom; apartments which have not been regularly occupied are apt to be damp." "You have some other reason for saying lam better out of it," I persisted, but Dr. Willis apparently did not hear me; for he began to talk about a coming local exhibition of autumn flowers, and to ask if we intended entering as competitors, the chrysanthemums in our garden having great reputation in the neighbourhood. My own little autumn blossom arrived that night —that fair sweet flower which was so soon to be planted in the garden above. The babe lingered with us but for a day, and for many weary weeks afterwards it was thought that I was to follow ray little one to the Silent Land. Gradually, very gradually, I crept back to life, thanks greatly to the devoted attention of my sister-in-law. Real natures come out in times of sickness and sorrow; in my invalid chamber I learnt to know Rhoda as I had never done before. I do not think any cloud, or doubt, or misunderstanding will ever come between us again; and my sad time of sick- ness and sorrow won for me a devoted and affection- ate sister, who, as the children say, "is to live in our house till we are all hundreds of years old." Poor old Sir John had died rather suddenly at last, and Frank, hastening home at once, unwilling to startle me by a telegram, arrived to find a little daughter newly born, a wife apparently dying. It was the younger, not the older, life which the "Reaper Death " gathered•at last; and Rhoda— unselfish, devoted Rhoda—who was the stay and support of us all throughout that 'terrible time, had the joy of seeing her toil in the sick room repaid by my complete recovery. As soon as I was able to travel, we went to take possession of the Scotch estate, where we have resided ever since, and two little ones have now come to fill the place of the short-lived babe who was born in the house in --shire. My long illness and the sorrow of our loss (the death of "only a baby " means a very real grief to its parents) put the memory of the strange sensa- tions I had experienced in that West Room almost out of my head, and Frank and Rhoda never en- couraged me to talk about the matter. But some ten years afterwards, at the "big gooseberry " time of year, I saw a paragraph in a Pape:: "Strange Discovery in a House in —shire ; the "house" no other than the one we had formerly occupied. It seemed that the persons from whom we had rented it had been compelled, like many other country folk nowadays, to sell the home which had been in the possession of their family since its erection, many generations ago. The new purchaser was making extensive alterations and impro4ements, in the course of which the hearthstone of the West Room wail removed—and there, just where I had sat when that nameless horror had seized me, was discovered the skeleton of a woman. Local tradition was now revived, and it was remembered that towards the close of the last century the house was occupied by a "squire " of somewhat dubious character, one of the original builders of the mansion. This person had, during the lifetime of his parents, led a wandering and dissolute life on the Continent, and when he returned to take possession of his property was accompanied by a French lady, to whom scandal asserted he was not allied by the ties of wedlock. The two led a disreputable existence together, shunned by the neighbours. A foreign servant, who was on most confidential terms with his master, had come with the lady, and the rooms at the end of the passage were devoted to the latter's accom- modation, the turret apartment serving as her boudoir. Darker rumours than even accusations of immor- ality were beginning to circulate in the neighbour- hood ; whispers that the squire was supplying traitorous information to France, with which country England was then at war. It was but rumour, a whisper; and the squire now began tel pay visits to London, where it was said he wae courting a young lady, whose relatives were pre- sumably ignorant of the circumstances of bill domestic arrangements in the country. But prying servants asserted that the attraction which tire° their master to London was detected by the "French damsel," and that, on his return front town on one occasion, an awful scene occurred between the pair; the foreign lady, who spok! English fluently, frequently exclaiming, 1001, enough to be heard through the locked door, thl7 "she knew what would hang him . . • au would be amply revenged." Next day the lady was not seen—nor, indssci,h' was she ever again beheld. M. Baptistei: French valet, announced that she had left house in a rage early in the morning after the quarrel overheard by the domestics, and that: neither he nor his master knew where she had , gone; but they supposed she had returned to France. No one in the neighbourhood was concerned too I inquire into the fate of a nameless and friendles foreigner, of more than questionable character; M. Baptiste locked up her deserted rooms, the squire paid a longer visit than usual to London, and returned after some months with a fair yolli wife, and settled down in his house as a refornie and "visitable " character. Good fortune, hul" ever, did not attend him; he broke his neck in the bunting-field within a year of his marriage, an.a the shock of his sudden death proved fatal to his young wife—and the expected heir. The house passed to distant cousins, from whose descendanti we rented it. No definite "ghost story" had ever been connected with the West Room, but—possibll from its associations with its first occupant—it had been out of favour with the squire's descendants, and it was sometimes whispered that disagreeable, dreams, if nothing else, visited the occupant 03 that chamber. As a very old resident in the neigh- bourhood, the local doctor of course knew of such traditions. Had a murder actually been committed in those apartments? Had (as I believe) the miserable, woman been slain to silence her tongue--surprise in her turret room by the valet, or his master, Og both; murdered, and then hidden beneath the hearthstone of her bed-chamber? Who can answel these questions now both victim and murdered have alike passed away? The newspaper spoke 01 the discovery of the skeleton as revealing an solved mystery," but I shall always fancy that know something of the facts of the case. Possibl some persons are more susceptible to "uncannY influences than others, possibly my then nervolle and highly-wrought condition made me something of a "medium " at that time; but I shall alwar believe that the story of the crime committed le those apartments was revealed to me during la brief occupancy of "The West Room." MR. GLAISTONE. THE LIBERAL MEMORIAL TO UR.

GLADSTONE. The platform that has been placed before the people is this—that without reference to the past we are carrying on a fight to day to which everybody in Ireland is invited to take part who vener- ates the deeds and the memory of the men of '9B, and who is faithful to their principles, those principles being the principles of Wolfe Tone and Lord Edward'Fiteeerald, which mean that Ireland is a nation entitled to make her own laws, and that she will never rest from 'her struggle until those rights are fully recognised (hear, hear, and anplausee) So that the three years during which this ginionist Government has been in office have done noth- ing to improve the position of the Irish people, and less than mottling to mitigate or destroy the national senti- ment (heer,.hear). TELEGIt A.PHING wiTaour WIRES. __— LORD KELVIN TELEGR &PBS FROM THE NEEDLES. FROM LIGHTHOUSE TO SHORE. MAncoen's ether telegraph has passed the experi- mental stage, and the success of the Bournemouth and Alum Bay scations has proved beyond a doubt the prac- ticability of his marvellous system for signalling through space without wires. In spite of the many experiments wnich have been recently made in Germany. Austria, France and in this country since Mr. Marconi first announced his discovery, which placei him miles ahead of any of his predecessors, vigorous attempts have been made by men of eminence in the scientific world to modify the apparatus and to get ahead of him ; but hitherto all such attempts have only proved how far behind Marconi are the experimenters in question. A DECIMAL CONVERTS 4 INTO 400! A short time ago" The Daily Telegraph" published a report that a French gentleman had signalled through over 400 miles by means of ether waves, but upon in- vestigation it turns out that a decimal point was omitted by some one in the report, and 4 miles were converted into 400. • I have read some extraordinary reports of Marconi's invention and the work he is engaged upon at the pre- sent time. On newspaper credited him with the dis- covery of ethe. ! Since January two wireless telegraph offices have been open for several hours daily under the imme bate super- intendence of Mr. Marconi, and the apparatus has been worked by the inventor's own experts, the employes of the Wireless Telegraph and Signal Company. One office is near the pier at Bournemouth and the other is at the Needles Flotel,Alum Beyen the Isle of Wight, about fif- teen miles foam the ' English Riviera.' Pelegrams have been sent in both directions for a period of six months, and the results have been of a most satisfectory nature. Recently the speed of signalling has been increased, and , the marks upon the Morse paper have been las superior than the signals I have rosived myself in byeone days when I have been working comparatively short sub- marine telegr.ph cables. Indeed, some of the signals received by means of Mr. Marconi's app trews would bear comparison with the dots and dashes registered by a Morse printer on an inland telegraph line. TELEGRAPHING ACROSS A TOWN. A few weeks age I had an opoortunity of testing this application between the flour mill and the jute works of I Messrs. Goolbedy at Clara, King's County, Ireland. The I buildings ar about a mile apart,ancl the installation was I ready for the transmission of messages in about an hour's time! Those persons wno have had anything to do with telegraph or telephone line construction will readily appreciate a system which will enable one to pot two important factories in electrical communication in an hour's time, to say nothon of the "way-leave" diffi- culties which beset the telephone engineer. During this practical demonstration I signalled a large number of messages, and the marks reeisterei were equal to the signals received by the aid of conducting wires in a postal telegraph office. LORD KELVIN USES THE INSTRUMENTS. Returning to the Bournemouth and Isle of White stations,a number of distinguished visitors have recently inspected these interesting offices. Not long ago Lord Charles Beresford, who uaderstagds the Morse code transmitted his own message acress the sea, and our great scientist Lord Kelvin, whose beautiful instruments made Atlantic telegraphy a commercial success, spent a considerable time in company with Lady Kelvin and Lord Tennyson at the Wireless Telegraph Company's Alum Bay Station. The distinguished party carefully examined the outside of the station, and witnessed the sending and receiving of several messages. Lord Kelvin sent " wireless telegrams "to Dr. McLean, his chief assistant in the Physical Laboratory of the University of Glasgow, to Sir George Stokes, at Cambridge, and to Lord Rayleigh and Mr. W. EL Preece. in London. The message to Sir George Stokes was S S follows : Stokes, Lensfield Cottage, Cambridge. This is sent, commercially paid, at Alum Bay for transmission through" ether." One shilling to Bournemouth and thence by Postal Telegraph fifteenpence, to Cambridge. —Kelvin.' Lord Tennyson'e mess Age WAS addressed to his nephew at Eton. 'Sending you message by Marconi's .. other" Telegraph Alum Bay to Bournemouth, paid commercially thence by wire. Very sorry not to hear you speak Your Thackeray to-morrow—Tennyson.' Lord Kelvin insired upon paying a shilling per message as a royalty for the uee of the Marconi system on all the messages he sent,wishing to show in this way his appre- ciation of the system, and to illustrate that it is already available for commercial purposes. A MESSAGE IN ITALIAN. The following day the Italian Ambassador, with three members of the Embassy staff, visited the Isle of Wight for the purpose of personally inspecting Marconi's Tele- graph. His Excellency expressed himself as highly pleased with his visit. and the success which has been achieved in so short a space of time. He sent amongst ether messages a Icing telegram by the aid of ether waves to sournemeuth for transmission to the first aide-de-camp of 'the King of Italy. As this dispatch of between forty and fifty words was all in Italian, it may be considered as a code communi- , cation, the language not being understood by Ms. Mar- coni's assistants at either station. The telegram was I received without a single error. INSTALLATION AT TEE HOUSE OF COMMONS. Some very interesting experimeres were recently cendunted between the House of Commons and St. Thomas's Hospital. One set of apparatus was in the small smokine-room juat off the terrace at the 'Rouse of Commons, and the other set was fixed in the Treastirer's 'house at the hospital. I At the Tlouse of Commons end a wire was led through the windowand attached to a short pole,which was tied to one of the lamp posts on the terrace a few feet from the window. At the hospital end the "vertical wire"was led from the window down to the top of a lamp pest, about six feet from the wall of the hospital. The time occupied in 'fixing both installations was under one , bout, and immediately after, the Sp.aker, Serjeant-at- Arms, and other Right Hon. and Hon. gentlemen came into the smoking-room to see the experiments. The lint message sent was The Speaker here. He wishes to 'know if the steamers passing are getting 'shocks.' The reply which came immediately was, 'No. but by our help they could receive messages,' which is ateritetrue. All the steamers would require would be a Marconi instrument fixed on board, when the telegrams might be received without any hitch or trouble. Sir 'Edward Clarke asked the hospital end, How high is your wire from the ground ?' About fifteen feet,' was the-reply. • The first news •of the resolution passed by the House of Commons in connection with the public funeral of the Rt. Bon. W. E.. Gladstone was conveyed wirelessly to St. Thomas's Hospital. The various mes- sages sent and receiveclwere all printed on the tape,' and afforded every satisfaction to those who witnessed the experiments at the ether end. Mr. Marconi hopes to establish communication be- tweenthe Isle of Wight and the coast of France erelong. And the Wireless Telegraph and Signal Co. are at present engaged in connectingSathlin Island,off the Irish coast, -with the mainland. During the course of an interview I had with Sir Howard Grubb, that gentleman expri Bassi a hope that communication between the Fastnet lighthouse near esaosenstown and the shore might be established by means of Marconi's system. Sir Howard promised to render what assistance he could to advance the accents- plish:nent of such an important installation. WAITING VERMISSION TOR EDDVSTONE LIGHTHOUSE. A few Lbws ago I was assured by soveral members of the Plymouth Town Council that the use of the Smeaton Tower on the Hoe might be obtained for a shore station Marconi' system could be installed in the Eddystone Lighthouse, about twelve miles distant. The people of the West of England are mvst anxious to have electrical communication between 'Plymouth and the Eddystone, and one philanthropic gentleman, Mr. Eobert Bally, of Torgrove, actually offered £3,001) out of his own pocket some years ago towards the cos s of a telegraph came. it now snly remains for the Trinity Brethren (or, rather, the Fog Office, firm all public telegraphs are under their contral)to give their consent for the appara- tus to be placed in Eddystone Lighthouse. Surely the Font (Mee will not hesitate to Wive assent. The value of such communication to the maritime world cannot be overestimated; indeed,it is no exaggeration 'to say that electrical communication between our shores; and our IigI3OOIIEWS and ships is a question of national import- ance.—Wastrars LYND, in the " auristian Wealf.."

STOCKTON IlEArn.

NOTES AND CO.IIMENTS. BY E.E. Let me offer in the first place a warm welcome to "Observer "—I hope !shall have for many years the pleasure of reading his notes

As I noticed, be referred to a report of the Stock- ton Heath School incident which appeared in the 'Liverpool Evening Express'. Let me draw his atten- tion to the following which appeared in the same paper on Friday. It will aff 3rd him and others food for mental digestion:—" Reference was made in our columns some time ago to an incident which had occurred in Stockton Heath Day School, when cer- tain children who came to the school with their hair in plaits and curl-papers neatly tied up had been sent home and informed that they must not return unless these plaits and curl-papers were removed. It was also stated that these children were taking part that evening in an entertainment and it was suggested that the incident arose out of the friction which existed between the school authorities and the promoters of the entertain- ment."

"Last week we received from another source a communication pointing out that our informant had not been in the possession of the whole of the facts of the case, and pointing out that the school managers had no choice but to act as they did, the Government inspector having written in the school log book that 'girls should not be allowed to come to school in curl papers. No girl would be allowed to attend a high school in such a state, tL lid there is no reason why public elementary schools should encourage middle-class slovenly habits.'"

"This was altogether another story, and vin- dicated the action of the authorities. But our original informant was not satisfied, and in view of public comment on the new phase of the case he made inquiries, and discovered that the action of the authorities was not the outcome of the school inspector's notice, but quite the contrary, the notice having been written by the inspector after the in- cident had been made public. So much for the ingenious story of the correspondent who under- took to put us right. The question is now being asked whether it is within the province of a school board inspector to order the exclusion of children because their hair is in curl-papers.""

It will be observed that in the notice stated to have been written in the School Log Book by the Inspector the words "middle class slovenly habits' are used.

Now it must be borne in mind that these chil- dren who were sent out of the school were in a clean condition. Not only do the parents state this but the School Authorities have admitted this. The Inspector could not have seen the children if the incident occurred before his visit, and before he made the entry in the school book.

RALF SCARED TO DEA.Tii. "I was half scared to death." A common expression. Everybody has used it many times. Ordinarily it means nothing; and it is almost an exaggeration. But there is a principle behind it, and a long list of facts which may well interest and alarm us. People have boon so soared that their hair has turned from its natural colour to white in a few hours. People have been scared into insanity, and in numerous in- stances they have been actually and literally scared to death. History teems with proofs of what I say; and Such a result is as clearly the effect of a natural law as Is death by a bullet through the brain or the heart. Lis- ten if you please, to the story of a child, who was half scared to death. Her mother tells it, and the incident as she relates it has since been fully verified. "Up to September, 1891," she says, "my daughter I Mary, now sixteen years old, was a bright, healthy girl. At that time she accidentally met on the road a tramp, whose appearance and language frightened her so badly that she ran home almost out of her series. After this she drooped and her health failed. She became weakly and delicate. Any effort soon exhausted her, and she would not play like other girls. She complained of a bad taste in her mouth, and a slimy matter adhered to her teeth. Her appetite forsook her, and she took only beef tea and milk. Even after such mild forms of nourish- ment as these the girl would go nearly distracted with pain in the side, the back, and around the chest. For this I applied poultices and hot bottles. Cold, clammy and unnatural sweats came over her, and she became pale !Ike a dead person. Frothy water ran from her mouth, and we wore unable to stop it. As she grew weaker large abscesses formed on her neck. a little later she was too weak to stand and I put her to bed. "For sir months she rentable i in bed without strength to move. I was obliged to lift her inlmy arms and:heat her back, when occasion reqeired. For the most part she lay quite listless as if under a load of sleep and weari- ness; but her rest was broken by pain. She wasted rapidly as if in a decline, and in two months lost one I atone in weight. She was now so light I could easily lift her. When relatives and friends called to see her she took no notice, and did not wish to hear them talk. Many times I thought she was dead, as she had no per- ceptible pulse, and I could not hear her heart beat. Yet tot water bottles and wine would revive her. "In this condition Mary remained:month after month, growing weaker, if that was possible. Na one thought she could much longer, and I tried to resign myself to parting with my beloved daughter. It made me ill to see her sufferings, and I felt tea years older duriug thosesis,anxious months. . "1 had one doctor after another to see her (seven doctors in all treated her), but she seemed no better. One after another the medical men gave her up as incur- able, saying they could do no more for her. A physician f rom Dublin (a specialist) when on a visit to Stranorlar Was brought to see my girl. He agreed with the other deetoes—tbat her case was hopeless. When we had abandoned all hope I chanced to read in a small book which had been loft at the house., about cases like Mary'; having been cured by Mother Seigel'a Syrup. lat once got a bottle from Mr. John Haat, the druggist. and began giving her this medicine. In a short time I noticed a slight improvement, and thereafter abs got stronger—slowly but sorely. When she had taken ten bottles she was well and strong. Since that happy time she has kept in good health. Everybody in the district wondered ..at the cure, con- Soldered it almost like a miracle, and asked what had done it. I told them all (including our Rector, the Rev. J. A. Crawford, who visited her regularly)' that Mary's recovery was wholly due to her use of Mother Seigel's Syrup, and that, save for this remedy, she would, in my opinion, be now in bee grave. You are at liberty to publish this statement and refer any enquiries to me."— (Signed) (Mrs.) Matilda McConnell, Stranorlar, near Londonderry, Co. Donegal, Ireland, August 31st, 1897. The facts in this case are public:property among th people of the vicinity, who corroborate the account of them as given by Mrs. McConnell. Among others we quote one letter 1—" I have known Mrs. McdCoenuneeedll the many years, and remember her daughter's illness. She informed me that Mother Seigel's Syrup he 1 girl after seven doctors had failed. Mrs. McConnell is a respectable lady, and her word can be implicitly relied on. The case is well known in the district. You can publish this letter if you wisb."—(Signed) John Raitt, Merchant, Stranorlar. Ireland, August 31st, 1897. I The fright received by little Mary McConnell—then to ears old—produced a profound nervous shock, which resulted in an arrest of the digestive functions, with succeeding symptoms as described. It was indeed a marvel that she recovered. A medicine capable of curing such a ease is tryand praise. Because of the trouble they passed through may the future be good to mother and daughter.

WARRINGTOI MUSICAL SOCIETY.

ANNUAL MEETING.-A. HANDSOME

PROFIT.

Mr. J. C. Parr, J.P., DL., presided at the annual meeting of the Warrington Musical Society which was held in Trinity schools, on Thursday last week. There was only a moderate attendance. At the outset, Mr. ATKIN (hon. secretary) read letters ot apology for non-attendance from Mr. John White, J.P ,and Mr. Roger C. Parr,the latter writing from Christ Church, Oxford. . . .

The SECRETARY in his report said before submitting his report for the season 1897-8, he felt he would not be acting in accordance with the wishes of the society generally and individually, if he did not refer to the loss sustained by the death of Mr. Rylands. In the early part of this rear the society lost its late president, and he might almost say its father, for ha thought he was right in saying there was no one left amongst them who could go back into the history of the Warrington Musical Society, and remember a time when Mrellylands' name was nct prominent in its records. His (Mr. Atkins) association with Mr. Rylands was, as was known to all present, at the end of this long period and for a few years only and in spite of his ad- vanced age and consequently less active life,his interest even to the last was as keen and great as ever, though naturally he could not make this as apparent as in the earlier an I more active years of his connection with the society. He felt that it would more fittinely come from a much older member of the Musical Society to speak to the worth of one so long connected with it,and it was naturally very little that he ()Ir.Atkin) could say. But the feeling remained that he would do wrong to let this, the only opportunity, though a seemingly Ide one, pass without voicing the society's tribute of respect for the memory of its late president and long supporter, Mr. John Rylands. A summary ani review of the work of the Society for the past season then followed. Continuing, the Secretary said that the membership list for the season closed showed a complete force of 227 of chorus and orchestra, being 77 sopranos, 51 contraltos, 24 tenors, 40 basses; total 192 in the chorus, and 35 in the orchestra, not including the additional professional help at the concerts, it still being necessary to fill up gaps in the orchestra at the perform- ances. The attendance cards showed a good average attendance of each section at the numerous rehearsals, though there was even yet something to be desired in the general regularity of the members at the practices, and he noticed at one of the concerts a thinness in the places allotted to the bass section which tie hoped would not occur again, (Laughter.) Without counting the one or two final rehearsals held in the Parr Hall, there were 37 practice meetings in all for the preparation of the works performed held in Trinity School, and the season cemmeneed on September 21st, 1897 and closed on March 23rd, 1893. It was not his:province to criticise the work of the past sea: son, and time was too short to say all he could or would like to but he wanted be admitted one or two remarks. First of all a step was taken by the nommitt ee in arrang- ing the season's prospectus on the 19'h of August last 1 eear which he thought they would allow had been fully justified by the results when they heard the treasurerce financial statement. He referred to the alteration and reduction of the scale of subscription and aamiseion prices to the concerts. Another pliet was th snieme of selling tickets on a bonus principle, if he might call it as by the members, and here he just wished to say how much obliged the c :mmittee and himself personally were to those who worked so hard, and espec- ially his thanks were due to the gentlemen who relieved him by undertaking the management of this sale as far as the chorus in its different sections was concerned. The band members alone bad sold £5 33. 63. for the "E.ijah" concert, £2 163. 63. for the " Messiah," £3 15s. for the February Bth concert, and £1 Bs. for March 23rd concert, making a total of £l3 3s. and this at a cost of one free two shilling ticket only on the whole lot. He fancied the generosity of members in foregoing their en- titled bonuses was great and pleasing in the extreme. Ile must be allowed leave to offer his personal thanks for the help and support and willing co-operation of all the officers and committee of the society. Thanks from himself were due to Mr. Reynolds, Mr. Caldwell, Mr. White, Mr. Newcombe and others whose help had been as invaluable as ever, and he had a feeling that unless there was a Mr. Crosaley there was a great doubt whether there would be a Musical Society at the present time. He could sefely say he could not and would not have tackled the difficulties they had gone through, had he not felt that with such a conduct or at the head of affairs they were bound to come right at the end. In conclusion, without taking the wind out of Mr. Reynolds sails he wished to add that now, if ever, the Warrington Musical Society was at the point where it might with reasonable pride consider its welfare assured, if, and this was the point, it could only ensure to itself by a complete and enthusiastic working each season, the same results musically and financially as it had attained during the season 1897 8. He firmly believed and sin- cerely hoped it was now as much, nay snore, in favour , than ever it was with the public of the town and neigh- bourhood, and it only remained to do good works well to secure the position it ought to bold as one of the oldest local institutions. (Applause). The CHAIRMAN said before he called upon the treasurer for his report he felt that he should be only re echoing the feelings of all when he emphasieed all that had been an judiciously said by the secretary on the loss they had sustained by the death of their late president. His (the chairman'e) earliest associaton with the society was in connection with Mr. Rylande,and be watched very often the keen interest he took in the performances. By his interest and thorough appreciation of music, Mr. Ry- lauds was of the greatest assistance to the society in its earlier days. He stuck to it through thick and thin, through good arid evil report, and it was with very great regret that they accepted Mr. Rylands' resignation when he was obliged through failing health to Insider it. His memory would ever be green amongst them and they all very much regretted that be was no longer with theno.(Hear, hear.) He thought he might compliment their honorary secretary upon the very interesting and exhaustive re- port which he had hero able to give them, he thought it contained many elements that ought to give them very keen satisfaction. Be fancied from what he had heard that they would have greater need for satisfaction when they had hoard the Treasurer's report. (Applause.) TICE TREASURER'S REPORT.

pen.

Mr.L.W.Ritynows then presented his annual financial I statement- At toe outset he explained that at the annual meeting last year it was gated that there was an adverse balance of £95 193 4d., but whea the accounts were closed it was found that the debt was reduced to £95 64. They therefore began the past , season with that deficit. The members subscriptions during the season just closed were £54 4s 61., against £46 12s. 61., in the previous season the subscription list amounted to £3 81 61. against £134 3.4. (Hear, ' hear). The reducdon of the subscription 3 to the centre and side gallaties had been successful. Instead of having 50 subscribers to the centre gallery and second reserved Feats, they bad dur- ing the past season 85. (Appause). The total number of subscribers during the season just closed was 305 against 237, the previous season. (Hear, bear.) The receipts from the sale of tickets and programmes amounted to £253 03.11 d. against a corresponding total of £192 10s. 41. The total income for the season was 46460 13s. 11d. against £373 ss. 10s. (Applause.) On the expenditure side they bad paid £834 Os. 61. for pro- fessional assistance, and after meeting all other expenses there was a balance on the year's working of £47 6s. 91. (Applause.) If they deducted that amount from the ad- verse balance of £95 63., they would see that the debt now owing by the society was reduced to £47 193. 3j, (Applause.) The CHAIRMAN thought the reports were eminently satisfactory, and he was sure they owed a debt of grati- tude to all those who had worked the s^ciety and pro- dueed such satisfactory results. The society was in a far better position than last season. It showed a vitality and go which augured well for the future. As soon an a society of that kind got into a firm position there was no telling what it might achieve in the future. Their thanks were due to the secretary and treasurer for the exhaustive reports they bad given them, and conducting the business of the society during the season. Ile was sure they would accord them a hearts' vote of thanks. Be had pleasure in proposing the adoption of the reports, and he would couple with it a vote of thanks to the secretary and treasurer for their untiring rffoits for the benefit of the Society. Mr. NEWCOMBE seconded the proposition and humour- ously referred to the difficulties met with by the secre- tary and Treasurer during the year.--The proposition was unanimously carried. Mr. ATKIN in responding said there were difficulties attending the office of secretary but when they got to the end of the year, and bad ouch a satisfactory report to show, the diffieulties seemed rather mall. He was grateful to them for the vote of thanks. and was glad of the kind support be always received from the members.

ELECTION OF PRESIDENT. Mr. R. T. FaxacLotrou said he had great pleasure in proposing the re-election of Mr. Parr as president for the ensuing year. (Applause.) He felt that the success of the Society was in a large measure due to Mr. Parr for the simple reason, that if it had not been for his generosity, they would not have had a hall in which to sing. The hall they previously time,' was not fit for a Society like theirs to slog in ; but the hall they had at the present time was very suitable indred, and the sue- CeSS of their concerts was in a large measure due to the fact that the audience could hear what they had to ren- der. He also thought that as long as Mr. Parr was their president the members would think they were being honoured by his occupying that position. Mr. FANSHAW said he had great pleasure in seconding the proposition. He was of opinion that if they had not had a good friend in their President the Warrington Musical Society would not have stood in the position it did that day. They owed much t 6 his generosity, From a musical point of view the town had profited by it. He was sure all the members were proud of working under

such a president as they had,and he believed that under his presidency they would gain further success. The resolution was carried with acclamation.

The CIIAIRMAN.in responding, said he-felt almost over» whelmeci with the kind references which had been made to his very poor servic,s. They must all understand that whatever benefit had accrued from what he had done,it must give him tenfold pleasure in seeing the pleasure it gave to other people. Ele believed the ball had been of benefit to tne town generally and especially to the musical portion of it. Previously it was almost impossible to have music properly represented, but now it was,and he hoped would be well represented by the Musical Society. It gave him very great pleasure to continue another year as president. Re almost wished they had selected a more energetic man for the position but as they bad sa many active members perhaps a. figure head was the bast for their president. (Applause.)

THN VICE PRESIDENTS. On the proposition of the Castnates, seconded by Mr. G. F. F. Watra,the followinz gentlemen were appointed vice-presidents ; The Mayor (Alderman Wright),Messrs. John Crosfield. A. Crosfield, J. A. Eielsten, James Fair. dough, John Fairclough. R. T. Fairclough, R. Garnett, Dr. Gornall, F. Monks, R. C. Parr, and John White.
