THE CONDUCTOR.

Mr. W. E. ATKIN proposed the - v-election of Mr. Crossley as conductor.—Mr. R. H. Si LCOCK seconded,and said they owed much of their success to the cvaductor. He did not thing they could have one who took a deeper interest in the society or displayed greater ability than Mr Crossley.—The proposition was unanimously agreed to.

RE-ELECTION Or SECRETARY AND TREASURER. Mr. NEWCOMBE said he bad pleasure in proposing the re-election of the retiring secretary—Mr. W. E. Atkin, and the retiring treasurer—Mr. L. Reynolds.—Mr. ECCLES seconded the proposition, which was carried unanimously . In acknowledging the vote, Mr. REYNOLDS said there was more en- couragement in taking office now than previ- iously had been the case. They seemed to be on the right tack, and were scoring a success. He did not take all the credit for that success although he held the posi- tion of treasurer. The chief credit he thought was due to their conductor who concerned himself so much in the internal work of the society, and was ably assisted by their worthy secretary. He must not overlook tbe very great assistance which had been rendered lor tho members themselves. Be was of opinion that more was done by them during the past season than in any prev- ious seas in, and it was a very encouraging feature that the members were willing to work to bring about a financial Success. (Applause.) Mr. A.TEIN also replied, and referred to the assistance the society had received from Mr. Caldwell, and also from Mr, G. F. F. Whits and Mr. Newcombe.

TruNics TO TICKET SELLERS.

Mr. Caossf.Ey on b-ing called upon by the Chairman to make a few observati nu, said he was very grateful to the members for the kind words which had been ex- pressed, and the hearty manner with which his name had been received. With regard to the work of next season he thought it was too soon to make any actual announcement. He had been asked by the committee to think over a scheme, but be had not yet come to any conclusion. He believed they knew the hall had been already engaged,and it bad been arranged to have four concerts on or very near to the corresponding dates of last season. The" Messiah" would be again given at Christmas time. The other works would have to be settled within a very short time. The report which had been presented that night was very favo rrable, and be thought they were entitled to feel gratified and jubilant over it. Ile, however, hardly thought they would be so successfal in the coming seasors.Last season they wero for- tunate to select works which were very go 3d,and at the same time still appealed to the public. 'racy were also very fortunate to get some really very good singers with good naenerross for instance,Dlr. Santlev. It was not al- ways possible to so arrange matters. However, his earnest endeavour would be that they should have an equally, if not more prosperous season, than they had had this. (Applause.) On tins proposition of Mr. Atklar, the chairman's ser-, vices were acknowledged, and Mr. Parr having replied the meeting terminated.

THE Unionist party has been 'Applogisine for Ritualism. placed in a false position by taking up the Benefices Bill and making 'szcuses for not yielding to the demand for fresh Teatraints upon the ritualising clergy. That ex- cuses are being made is undeniable, and that they re very distasteful to many Churchmen on the nionist side is equally demonstrable. The ties (11 party restrain the majority of the Unionist Party, however,from making declarations and pro- ions that would meet the situation. It is said tht. at the suggested precautions would be so ineffec- -Ivs) that they are no part of the Benefices Bill, that the law is sufficient as it stands, and that ranch must be left to the discretion of Bishops;the and so on. Mr. BALFOUR 'S "en disposed to minimise the evil. I°W, what was the Benefices Bill introduced for if not to reform away abuses It was the project Of the Church Committee, and that Committee has for its leading spirits two of the sons of the lIREMIER, without whose aid the Government Would have left the measure in private hands, sad it would have bean defeated, as on former °cessions. The occasion is thus one in which the Whole Huse of Commo!ts is interested. One of t.he intentions of the bill is to increase the power of the Bishops, in order to prevent scandals con- ni:cted with tia silo oZ next presentations. ut ever effort to make such scandals impossible to occur is met by the plea that the Bishop must be left a discretionary _Power. Pi "may" d 3 a nu nbe-2 of things. When it was proposed to transform "may" into "shall ' the whole of the power of the Government Was brought to bear to crush one of their own supporters. When it is suggested that the Bishop should refuse institution to well-known ritualisers two lines of apology are suggested. The first is that he can do it now; the second is that the bleasure has no decided bearing on doctrine and

tibial. How, then, it mty be asked, do so many e2trome young men obtain livings? Either be- cause the law is not obeyod by the Bishop, or be - t'ause the sense df the Church is that it would be inconvenient for the Bishop to refuse 10 institute. The Church has been under-' mined by permitting this discretion, and therefore it ought to be taken away. /tshops have become as lax as the clergy, and so titualism has grown bolder. The Bishops have been squeezed and defied. The law of the land is disobeyed. What, then, is the value of the Church established by law if the law about doctrine and ritual and obedience to episcopal 4Uthority can be so successfully defied? That is the layman's question, and the Attorney-General's 411sWer is ambiguous. He regrets, and he refuses !Lei:l°4B3,st persons who wish to attack the root of e MiSchief. By empowering and directing the C;r"ll°Ps to refuse institution to these law- ieakers the Church would be reformed td°es not look as if it would ever be reformed in ether way. The Bishops go to sleep, the -Kltualists push here and push there, and when cl.biections are made it is said that " No Popery " anarrow, a bigoted, and a party cry. The country will, we hope, watch very carefully the controversy which is going on, and the timid, quibbling, and apologetic attitude adopted by the nionist party. Many things have been sacrificed to Maintain the Irish Union. If the Protestantism 13t the Church of England is now about to be offered up in the same way, the country will have t° speak its mind.

THE VICE-PREUDENT of the Sir John Committee of Council on Education. Gorst. is just now the object of a large attount of severe criticism. The friends of Volun- tary schools do not enjoy the thrusts he has given 4t their religious education, at the attendance of their scholars, and the efficiency of their teachers. teast of all do tney relish the intimation that ltotil rural schools are placed under some better 14;1thority than school associations matters are no to be very much improved. They do no llllite see what he is driving at ; and we confe3s 1;a sharing in this perplexity. What would have ? He is called upon to adminis- :▪ et a double system, and he professes to be friend of the denominational side of it and great admirer of mall School Beards. •Lit" has had to defend the system whenever it has been attacked, and his own attacks upon it are therefore the more extraordinary to its friends. What he feels himself compelled to do is to array the most damaging facts he can in a form that makes them effective for platform purposes. Yet e has not made any proposals for effecting an alteration, and he has not seriously attempted to :tilighten the House of Commons and the country ri the working of the Voluntary Associations and v Special grant. On these points there is a strong "rarnand for information, and it has not been sup- Puled. in what character, therefore, does Sir JOHN wish to figure? Is he an educationist, m'scontented with the system he has to administer, With the amount of power he is permitted t exercise and with his position generally under a 'tho has more than:once overridden his views?

===== 0002601_18980624_art0066.txt | included=true | included =====
That interpretation is quite consistent with some of the facts of the case, if it fails to explain them all. On the other hand, there is the more per- sonal view of the case. It is often said that Sir Joarr GORST is ill at ease, that he is not satisfied with the position into which he was thrust because no better one offered, and that he has been anima- ted by a desire to make everything about his De- partment as uncomfortable as possible for every- body. There are doubtless influences at work, pushing him this way and that way, in accordance with the views of two or three schools into which the Government is divided on the subject of national education. Yesterday he was against Board schools; to-day he seems adverse to Voluntary schools. At one time he thought our system was too cen- tral; at another he seems to complain that it is not central enough. All these matters are very puzzling, and they provoke curiosity,criticism,and even disgust. It is usual to say that he is so candid that he must acknowledge the existence of awkward facts. It is now being asked why he is not as candid about his own position ? I t is most unsatisfactory. Daes he want more power? Is he hampered by the clerical influences which led to the special grant? Has his view of the work- ing of the Voluntary system convinced him that it cannot be maintained ? If the clergy cannot keep their schools going in an efficient state, and they will not bear their customary burdens, they must expect severer treatment in the future.

• A LETTER, perhaps even more The Mannine of the important than that of Sir A. ' Mercantite Marine. B. Foitwoon —because the as- sertions in it are backee up by argument—has been contributed to the " Daily Chronicle " by Mr. G. ELLIOTT BRO.-UWE, president of the Lon- don General Shipowners' Society on the extensive employment of foreign seamen on British Ships. His first argument is in refutation of the belief that the foreign seaman is employed because he is cheaper than the British seaman, and satisfied with inferior food. This he denies, and backs up his statement with the convincing argument that thea v paid to seamen, irrespective of their ti ty. are "the wages of the port," while nlouaagieis the food supply to each and every A,B.is regulated by the Board of Trade and examined by the Board's inspector. The theory of the choice of foreign ers before Eaglishineu on the ground of econ- omy is therefore exploded. There is no competi- tion in prices,—which would indeed be a serious matter, but the labour of either S3indinavian or Briton is procurable at the same rate of remuner- ation. Mr. BROUWE thou goes on to sh )w why the foreige searneu is so numerous in the mercan- tile marine, and in doing so he practically corrob- orates Sir A. B. Foeveoon's charge of lab week. As a rule he says, "they are better sailors than our own, being steadier and more subservient to dis- cipline than the majerity of our eeemen." Cer- tainly from all ac: mints the British seaman has vastly deterieriated. Then there is the scarcity of men " worth their salt," and Mr. Bitom- eoe attributes this to the nursery for British seamen having nearly dis ap- peared with the gradual ellecornent of the sailing ship. Here we have the source of supply limited, and the inferiority of our sailors in some impor- tant respects, as reasons for the preferment of foreigners. And when in addition to this, the correspondent of the "Daily Chronicle" advocates the compulsory carrying of apprentices on every steamet above 700 tons not register, and the graduation of certificates so that the merchant service may offer better epenings to boys of superior education and intelligence, by so doing he admits that the " foreign element " owes its pre- sence largely to the fact that Englishmen do not seek employment on our ships. We have not to go far to arrive at a conclusion why it is that English men and youths do not seek employment at sea with the same zest as in the past. The conditions are not the same. With the abandonment, to such a large extent,of masts and sails, "life on the ocean wave has become almost as much a hurry- scurry as existence on land. Fewer men are re- quired for the navigation of a steamer, and be- side the shortening of the trips abroad which re- duces the romantic attraction of life at sea, there is a scarcity of the one-time jovial companionship. These may be sentimental reasons, but they have some weight when it is probably sentiment and not necessity which would impel a British youth in even decent circumstances to go to the sea for a living. Foreigners seem content to work under such conditions without considering them a drawback and thus willingly accept employment where Englishmen reject it. The difficulty seems to be in making the conditions of employment and of promotion such as will draw men from this country who have something more than physical ability,and in thus bringing the foreigners in to competition with Englishmen of higher training, make it more difficult for them to find work on our ships. While practical solutions t • the difficulty are not readily forthcoming, it ceases to be such a problem why foreign seamen are so numerous in the mercantile marine.

Emperor William has again been speaking in public, and has • vm utterance to some character- istic opinions. lit, is the ruler of a great nation, but one forgets this when ho opens his mouth and speaks. "Out of the mouths of fools cometh wis- dom," 'tis said, bat this is by no means an invari- able rule, and while we should have some reluc- tance in placing Emperor William in this category, we cannot help but be struck with the trivial and superficial nature of his utterances. There is noth- ing to denote wisdom about them,and his latest al- lusion to his beloved army as "the most important inheritance left him by his graudfather and father," is not one calculated to evoke the admiration of the German people,or the respect of other nations. It is nothing but a travesty of imperial duty to thus talk of the army. Germany is not all bar- racks and battlefield, nor are the Germans soldiers to a man, but it is plainly the army first, and the country's weal anywhere or nowhere. Such Royal devotion to militarism may be a source of gratifica- tion to the army, but even this will be tempered by the Kaiser's pronounced expectation of "uncon- ditional, iron, and blind obedience." Then again Emperor William enlightens his subjects on a monarch's duty to the theatre. After some high souled remarks about " the school of idealism," and " the preservation of the highest spiritual qualities of our noble fatherland," His Majesty proclaims that "it is the duty of a monarch to interest himself in the Theatre." It may have been noticed that most monarchs do frequently interest themselves in the theatre, but for some reason hitherto unsought and unexplained. Now we learn that it is from a sense of duty. Duty's paths, after all, may sometimes lie in very pleas- ant places, and the Emperor's explanation opens up an endless vista of now duties which one could stoically, even cheerfully fulfil.

A BOY BURGLAR AT RUNCORN,

On Thursday Morning oefore blri;rrd. -R. Norman, and H. Taylor, a boy named Frederick Harding, resid- ing with his father in Shaw-street was brought up in custody charged with breaking into the house of Mr. Robert flashy. 12. Shaw-street, and stealing therefrom the sum of 2s 71. May Basler, wife of Mr. R 'bait Bag.. ley gave evidence to leaving the house on Wednesday, and also the sum of 2a 41 in a glass in the kitchen cup- board. Upon her recurn she found the house had tom broken into and 2s 71 taken out of the glass. The husband gave evidence to finding the boy, and of his admitting the theft. He had locked the money in an empty bows in Shaw-street.—P.C. Gregory said he apprehended the prisoner that morning and in answer to the charge he said, " I took them dr. Gregory, but hope you'll let me off this time." rhe officer stated tbat he found some ingenious appliances (produced), for opening doors and window catches. The father expressed a desire that the boy should be sent to the reformatory, but the magis- trates said they did not feel justified in filling the reformatories with boys, who might be better if taken in hand.—Prisoner who pleaded guilty was soundly cau- tioned and sentenced to receive six strokes with the birch.

===== 0002601_18980624_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
for Mr. Dillon to break his journey at Warrington as he was en route for Dublin to attend the Wolfe Tone celebrations, and also that Sunday is the most convenient day for local Irishmen. At any rate the Irish people in Warrington and district attended in large numbers and paid their ad- mission fees willingly in order to hear thei:. Parliamentary leader. The mooting, very natur- ally and properly opened with a vote of condolence to the family of the late Mr. Gladstone. Mr. Dillon in seconding the vote, reiterated and amplified the views of Irishmen respecting the deceased states- man whichhe so fittingly and eloquently expressed in the House of Commons. He also took occasion to press home the fact that the universal chorus of lamentation, and unparalleled admiration from both friends and opponents of the greatest and wisest of British statesmen now passed away. was in itself a testimony to the wisdom of the Irish cause to which Mr. Gladstone devoted the later years of his life.

In his subsequent speech Mr. Dillon, as was per- haps only to be expected, dealt almost entirely with Irish affairs. The only time on which he took occasion to deviate from the Irish path was when he referred to foreign affairs. He poked fun at the blast of high-sounding trumpets which her- alded the entry of Lord Salisbury into the Foreign Office and the fanciful predictions which were made concerning the strong Unionist Government. After a three years' experience, he believed, the people of England were getting "pretty sick" of this great and strong government. "Never did England stand lower before the world," he declared, " than she does at present." Mr. Dillon's conclusions of the dissatis- faction to be heard on all hands with the present Government—" even in that palace of truth : the Smokeroorn of the House of Commons "—were that the time is not far distant when there will be an appeal to the country, an event, which he predicted was nearer than some people thought.

Mr. Dillon is nothing if not an optimist with regard to Irish affairs. With him Home Rule is far from being dead; it is very much alive. His speech was full of confidence, encouraged as he said he was by outward signs of enthusiasm amongst Irishmen, such as he bad never seen for many a long day. There are signs that the Irish in England, and in their native land are as deter- mined as ever to secure Irish liberty. After a graphic description of the condition of Ireland at the present time Mr. Dillon proceeded to carefully analyse the policy of Mr. Gerald Balfour" of killing Home Rule by kindness." He clearly con- trasted the spirit of Mr. Balfour in dealing—or refusing to deal—with the famine in the. West of Ireland, and the unblushing bribery of Irish land- lords to the tune of £300,000 a year in order to get their consent to the Irish Local Government Bill. Despito the difficulties which beset the Irish cause, Mr. Dillon looks forward to the future with perfect confidence on one condition, and that con- dition is that the Irish are united among them- selves. To secure their liberty the Irish people must be united, and this was the subject of the last public letter Mr. Gladstone ever wrote, and in which he . made his last profession of his unshaken fidelity to the Irish cause.

It is a very prevalent error that anybody can manage the affairs of a newspaper but tho man destined by training and circumstances t do so. We have frequently had this exemplified in our own experience, and wondered greatly at the colossal self-assurance of people possessed of the idea that to control the reins of a newspaper is the easiest task on earth, and just the one thing they could do to perfection. And that we are not alone in our sufferings from this class of busy-body is shown by the action of the editor of the" Gwelo Times," who quits the field in distraction and hurls the following parting shot at his tormen- tors in particular and the world in general: —"We have come to the conclusion that to edit, print, and publish a newspaper, be storekeepers, newsagents, book-keepers, and account collectors, be authorities on everything in tha heavens above or the earth beneath, and take advice (!) from every member of the community who considers he has a latent genius for running a newspaper, is too much for the health, wealth, or future existence of two ordinary civilised beings. So we do not feel sorry to relinquish control."

SUICIDE 91 A WIDNES WOMAN.

On Monday at Widnes, Mr. Jones, deputy coroner, held an inquest on the body i of a woman named Lillian Evans,4B years of ago,residing n Bedford-strect,Widnes, who died on Saturday from the effects of having taking carbolic acid. The deceased went Out shopping, when she purchased a quantity of carbolic acid. On arriving home she immediately went upstairs, and was follovvd by her daughter, but the deceased had already taken the poison before her daughter could prevent her. Remedies were at once administered, but without offset. The de- ceased had suffered from a bad leg tor some time, and had often said she " wished the Lord would take her way."—The jury returned a verdict of " suicide whilst in unsound mind caused through suffering."

STARVATION AT WIDNES. A.t inquest at Widnes, yesterday, revealed a shocking condition of home life. Catherine Mulligan. aged 55, died suddenly on Wednesday morning in a bou=e in North-street. The house was utterly without furniture, and the only beddiag, w.s a big of straw, upon which the deceased, her husband, and daughter, aged 22, had slept for three weeks. AU, C Instable Worsfold stated, were in a filthy condition. Tne deceased had had little food for days. A verdict of heart disease accelerated by starvation was returned.

CANON WOOD ON RELIGIOUS EDUCATION IN THE NATION.

On Sunday the annual sermons in aid of the funds of the Runcorn National Schools were preached in the Parish Church. In the morning the Rev. Canon Holing (vicar of Great Bailivorth), preached from the text, " What manner of child is this ?" He said that day they were asked to give liberally towards the support of the National Schools, hut though they were asked to do ' an he would not have them forget that there was a better way of supporting the cause, and that wag by annual Subscriptions : but, in whatever way they gave their support, they knew that they were supporting institu- tions that were doing real good.—ln the afternoon a fiewer service was held in the church and was well attended, when an appropriate address was delivered be the Rev. W. H. Stables. The floral offerings of the children were most liberal, and subsequently were dis. tributed amongst the sick of the town and district. The Rev. Cation Wood occupied this pulpit in the even- ing and delivered an address, placing the work of the Nttional Schools high in the place it shinld occapy 11l the cation. They were indebted to the voluntary system for the tone of Christianity that pervaded the nation. They might talk of haying the schools of the nation upon One common basis ; but ti,e result would be detrimental to the nation's morality and well-being. It was said by the opponents of the voluntary system that there were good religious teachers in the Board schools, and he seas pleased to learn that there were,. But to trace those teachers. They could be traced to the denominational schools and collcges where they had tecelved their edu- cational foundation—where they had had instilled in them the antigd principles that they possosseiMe traced them from infancy—lroca the g:tholl it the iiegui; and When they came on of college they—if they entered into the services sf the 8:hoot Board,gave their services conscientiously—they' could no: do other .vise—to teach the minds of those entrusted to them ,aiitia that know- ledge they themselves p.,sses;erl.Te;co aw ,v the voluntary system and it would only be a matter nt time before they would hays, no Sllch conscientions tetcherA at all. He commended the cause to their notic and concluded by giving some inter Wine; statist,cs in support of his remarks. The choir sang the anthem " The Lord iA K!1.17, " with their usual good taste. Liberal offertories were received a: all the services in aid of the echool funds.

WESTON.

PARISII COUNCIL

TIE PROPOSED NEW ROAD FELON WESTON TO RUNCORN. At a meeting of the Weston Parish Council held at the Church el.ohools on Friday evening last week, pre,ided over by the Rev. Canon Maitland Wood, the qneetien of the proposed road from Weston to Runcorn was con. sidered.—Mr. J. Ball said the committee appointed at the last meeting met the representatives a the Urban District Council at Runcorn, and discussed their pro- posal with reference to the making of a road to Weston Point. The representatives or committee of the Urban Council pointed out that they were prepared to buy the land and make the road as far as the limit of the Runcorn boundary, which would leave the Weston township a length of about 400 yards to make. It was stated that the cost of making the Weston portion of the road would be £3OO. It was mentioned during the interview that the money could be borrowed at 24 per cent, and the repayment spread over a period of 30 years. This would mean an expenditure for Weston of £l6 per annum, or would be equal to a charge of a halfpenny in the £ on the rates.— Mr. Ball explained that the route proposed was down Percival-lane, through the railway arch, by Swieten's Farm, along a portion of Cock and Hen-lane, past the easterly side of the Salt Works, and along the front of Mr. Clark's cottages to the South-road, Weston Point. Mr. Ornie complained that the proposed road was not the best and most direct they could get. He sugeested that a nearer route wield be through Pickow Farm to Cock and Hen-lane, and straight through the Salt Works.—lt was observed that this route was aban- doned because of the difficulty in getting land.—Mr. Tudor asked whether anyone had ascertained if they could have the land to make the road marked red. Re thought they ought to be satisfied on this point before they proceeded further. He would move that a com- mittee be appointed to wait upon the owners and get to know definitely whether the land was available.—Mr. Orme seconded.—After some further discussion the motion was carried, and the following gentlemen were appointed as the committee. Messrs. Allen, Ball, and Tudor.—The suleject of the representation of Weaver Ward (Weston Point) was also oreached, and the criair- man said he had writtea to the Rev. D. S. Bowen, and Messrs.R. R.Clarke,and F. W.Clark,asking them to serve as representatives of the Weaver Ward, but they replied firmly declining to have anything to do with the matter. —On the motion of Mr. Sorn-rville it wis Unanimously I decided that -he clerk take step; for c Aline another Parish meeting so as to get the divisien properly repre- sented.

WESTON FETE AND FAIIt.

A SUCCESSFUL VENTURE.

The Weston Fete and Fair was brought to a most successful issue on Thursday and Saturday last. Owing te the weather being unfavourable on the Saturday, however, some of the sports will be held over for a future date; and on Teursday for a like reason the Sale of Work was held in the schoal at Weston, where the Maypole dcnce and the action drill were repeated. As has already been reported the fee and fair was promoted with the object of raising the sum of .£20() in aid of the beautiful edi- fice, now knovva as the "Church of St. John the Evangelist," and of which a description was given in these pages upon the occasion of the laying of the foundation stone, and again upon its consecra- tioa by the Lord Bishop of Chester, and we heartily congratulate the promoters upon the success they attained. Through the untiring energy of the Rsv. W. R. Stables, M.A., who has been in charge of the church a; Weston for some time, the villagers may now rejoice in that they have a church of their own, and as his lordship the Bishop said upon the occasion he addressed its first congregation, " Really a most beautiful church." But to return to the Fate and Fair it was an unqualified success and accemplished its object.. Thanks to the amount of energy and enthusiasm infused into the undertaking, and the kind and generous support met with on all hands. Its object may be --aid to be fully attained. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

AGGRAVATED ASSAULT ON A WM.-A SEPARATION

ORDHR GRANTED.

At the Warrington Borough Police Court on Friday, before the Mayor and other magistrates. a labourer at Daltem Forge named John Smith, of 28, Hoyle-street was charged with assaulting and beating his wife on the 14th of June.—Be pleaded guilty.—Mr. Unswerth,solici- tor, who represented the complainant stated that the parties had been married for over 20 years. Latterly he had given way to habits of intemperance, and had commenced beating his wife. The beating commenced last summer, and so late as March last to beat her severely and continued drinking for about two months at a spell. On Tuesday last he struck her on the side of the head. She ran out into the lobby and he followed her and struck her there' He afterwards went into the kitchen and threatened to throttle her. He had also threatened to throw a lighted lamp at h-r, and she was in bodily fear of him. There were five children, and the complainant asked for a separation order of 7s. 61. a week. She would keep the children.—The Clerk said that before the magistrates granted a separation order there must be evidence of an aggravated assault.—The complainant was then called and stated that her husband struek her on the face and also on the body. She had scarcely slept since in conse- quence. He gave her three knocks on the back of the head. She also spoke of previous amaults by the de- , fendant.—He had now been drinking continually for three months.—Evidence in corroboration of the as4ault was given by Mary Ann Healey, Lilford-street.—The de- fendant when asked to speak in his own defence said he did not want to say anythiog. The magistrates could do what they liked.—The Mayor said it was a very painful case. It was a great pity that the defendant had given way to drink. The magistrates had decided to commit him to prison for one month with hard labour, and would grant a separation order, allowing 7s. per week.

MON& ROWDTISM AT TOWN'S .END.

On Monday before Alderman Harrison and other magistrates at the Warrington Borough Police Court, Margaret Flanagan, 4, Silver-square; Mary Griffiths,62, Oliver-street; and Sarah Hardman, 15, Silver-street,were summoned for committing a breach of the peace by fighting in Silver-street on the 12'h inst. Police constable Bap said he was on duty in Winwick-street when he received a complaint of a disturbance in Silyer-square. He went there and saw about 200 people, in the midst of whom were the three defendants, who were fighting. He rut them into their respective houses, and told thvn they would be reported. At the time of the dis- turbance the people were cemine out of an adjoining place ef worship.—The Chief Constable said rows were going en the whole of the day, and pelicemen were con- tinually going there on account of the conduct of these women.—Defendant Flanagan said that Jane Johnson (a woman previously charged with another offance) was not in the row.—Alciermen Harrison said defendant had no right to instruct anyone as to his duty. She would have to be put down . Defendant; Whatever the Lord sends I n ill take as a gift.- -The Chief Constable said Flanagan had been before the Court on 20 occasions. The other women were now making their first appearance.—Flanagan was alai charged with having been drunk and disorderly in Silver- square on the 10th of June, and thecae having been proved she was fined 104. feed costs.—For committing a breach of the peace the three women were bound over o keep the peace for six months, and were ordered to pay tbe cost.—While being removed to the cells Flan- agan behaved very violently. As she was being re- moved from the dock she shouted at the top of her voice, and said she was sorry the grand old man had left that corner (indicating the seat of the Chief Con- stable). There was justice then, but there was no justice now. They could give her another seven days. She had been before the Beach 20 times but had never said a wrong word. Samuel Hunt did give justice ta the widows and orphans for 30 years, but there was no justice now. Defendant continued shontine until she was out of hearing.—The Chief Constable : She is drunk now. THEFT OF WATER EfF.N'S EGGS FROM THE PARK. A nov.rone years of are, named George Barton, living with his parents at 42, Nicholson-street, was brought be- fore the Warrington Borough Bench on Monday morning charged with stealing from Bank Park on the 13th Jnne, seven water hen's eggs. He pleaded guilty.—The Chief Constable said the information was laid under the park bylaws. It appeared that in the ornamental water in the park there were geese water hen., which were intro- duced by the Town Clerk. On the night of the 13th June the park-keeper saw a hen's neat in which there were seven eggs. The following morning it was found that the :wee had been stolen. Thenarvlrevepsetrr and thing occurred. A woman came to the park-keeper gave him five water hen's eggs, stating that she had found them in the possession of her boy. It was at once theught teat these were the eggs which bed been stolen. Information was given to the police but as a result of inquiries it was found that these were not the eggs which had been stolen. They were Pgqg which had been taken at Strettnn on the previous day, and had been given to this boy by some others. It was, therefore, proved that he was not the culprit. The Town Clerk had a nlacard printed offerine £2 reward for in- formation which would lead to information respecting the person who had committed the theft. The result wee that Mr. Barton, the father of the defendant, wrote a letter to the authorities at Vie Town gall, stating that his boy had taken tha eggs. Be.of enurse,was not sok- ing the rewarg in giving the information. He (the Chief Constable)was asked by the Town Clerk not to press the case.—Mr. Barton said be knew nothing about the affair until Tuesday. His wife told him, and after he had seen the boy he wrote the authoritiss.—Mr. Talbot said the lad had made a statement to the effect that on Sunday afternoon he, with several other boys, went into the Park, and he was shown the rest and eggs. On Mondey be went with two others and took the eggs. They shared the eggs amongst them. They afterwards smashed them by throwing them against thewall.—Defeudant was fined in the costs, 31. 61.—Councillor Shaw Green thought the result of the ease should he made public, In the game manner as the railway companies published the cenvnne obtained in their cases. ASSA.IILT OF L POLICEMAN.—EIRAVY FINS.

At the Warrington Police Court on Tuesday morning before Mr. Robert Garnett (in the chair) and Mr.Toomas Birchall, Thomas Grady, of Wharf-street was charged with assaulting P.C. Carter in Msrsey-strest at 12.30 on Monday atternoon.—James Grady, of 3, Smith-street, was charged with obstructing the officer in question in the execution of his duty. The former pleaded guilty and the latter not guilty.—P.C. Carter said that about dinner time on Menday he saw a crowd at Bridge-foot. The two prisoners had been fig,hting and he followed them up. Thomas Grady struck him in the face without provocation. James Grady got hold of him by the right arm and pulled him away. Witness got the assistance of a bystander Mr. Russell and they were taken to the Police Station.— Jchn Edward Leach, working at the Gas Works, cor- roborated. Be malted the officer.—Mr. Hassell. cab proprietor, said be was in Mersey.st., and saw Thomas Grady strike the constable. Witness took hold of the prisoner James Grady, who was quiet. He saw the constable and the other prisoner on the ground.—The Chief Constable said it was lames Grady's 7th appear- ance and Thomas Grady's 4h appearance.—The Chair- man : The Court is very mach obliged for the assist- ancn rendered by these two witsesses.—Mr. Birchen : Yes, it is very brave of them. The Chairman : We mean to help, as far as we are able, the officers of the law in the execution of their duty, and I do think this assault is a most aggravated one. The Bsnch fined Thomas Grady £5 and costs or in default six weeks' imprisonment with hard labour, and James Grady 103. and costs or 14 days.

Jones said the Board of Trade had taken every step to Inform itself as to the circumstances and progress of the dispute in the South Wales coal trade.—Sir B. Gourley asked whether the rumour was correct that the usual naval manreavres were to be dispensed with this year.—Mr. Gosehen said the instructions sent to the ports were in the fallowing terms:" In view of the continued serious character of the strike amongst the South Wales collieries, it has been decided that it would be expedient to trench upon the reserves of coal for the naval manoeuvre,, and the Custom- ary" partial mobilisation of the fleet will therefore not take place. The Channel Squadron and the coastguard squadron would cruise as usual. The Channel Squadron consisted of eight battleships and six cruisers, and the reserve squadron of six battleships and three cruisers. Lest there should be any exaggerated idea in reference to the Admiralty stock of coal. he might add that they had never been in pos- session of a larger amount of actual coal than at the present tnoment,and this measure was taken as a mattter of prudence. TERRIBLE DISASTER AT THE LAUNCH

OF A BATTLESHIP.

===== 0002601_18980624_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
200 PEOPLE THROWN INTO THE THAMES. GREAT LOSS OF LIFE.

_ A terrible disaster at tbe launch of a vessel for the Royal Navy—equal only to the accident to the Daphne, in the Clyde, iu July, 1883, when 124 lives were lost— occurred on the Thames at Blackwell on Tuesday, on the occasion of the launch of the battleship Albion. from the yard of the I hames Ironworks and Shipbuilding Company. Special interest centred in the event not only by reason of the circumstance that the ceremony of christening was to be performed by the Duchess of York, arid that the Duke of York had come specially from the Crescent at Portsmouth for the occasion,but by the fact that the Albion is the largest addition to the navy ever launched in the Thames, where for many years past the shipbuilding industry had been in a decaying, if not moribund condition. The result was that there was a great crowd to witness the ceremony. Everything ap- parently had passed off happily and successfully, and cheers were being raised in celebration of the event, when, out of sight and unknown to their Royal High- nesses and the other distinguished visitors, a staging which had been erected around a Japanese warship le course of erection was swept away by the huge wash rallied by the Albion as she reached the water,and some 200 were suddenly immersed in the seething water.o:ber lestimates nay as many as 300 were swept away, but at all events there was great loss of life, and up to seven o'clock at least 30 bodies had been recovered.

George Cooke, a sturdy young shipwright, who had been at the yard from the beginning of tne ceremony, was able to give the representative of the Press AS9O- - a very clear narrative of what occurred.fie, with his brother, Frederick Cooke, was standing high up on one of the stages of the Japanese war vessel Shikishima, which is being built by the company lower down, and not many yards to the right is a creek. Across this creek was a wooden and unornamental bridge with the word clearly painted on it in large letters " Dangerous." Notwithstanding this, between twv and throe hundred people were crowded on it. Still further on to the right was a blank wall, and then an opening through which the Albion was expected to appear. Suddenly there was great shouting and cheering, and the huge vessel shot stern first into the water, " Then," said young Cooke, " there was a shriek of horror," for, as the Albion swashed along, and nearly touched the Opposite bank, the water rose in a huge and frightful backwash, and, swirling at least four feet above the bridge near the Skikishima, swept all the people upon the structure into the river. In a moment this part of the river, which could not be seen where the Royal party had stood, was a struggling mass of drowning people—mostly women and children. Nor could anything at first be done to save them, for some boats and tugs which were on the other side of the dock could not reach the spot until the Albion had floated into her berth near the Slukishima. When once she had gone by, however, the men on the tugs and tho boats went to work with a desperation little short of mar- vellous, and but for their grim entue,ty few would have suevived to tell the tale. Cooke, continuing his narra- tive, said—"As soon as the bridge with Ihe people on it had collapsed, and the wave had gone down,my brother and I took off our cats and waistcoats, and jumped in- to the struggling mass. I am a good swimmer, and have saved lives previously, and I was too excited to think of the danger. I kept diving underneath the spars, and anywhere where I thought a laxly mi4ht be found, and my brother and I brought up five people, three of whom were alive and two dead. One of the three rescued alive, however, succumbed after being landed. One woman pulled me underneath the water, and I should probably have been drowned,but assistance was at hand, and we were hauled on just in the nick of time. The number of victims stands at 34, of whom 32 were drowned, and two died in hospital alter having been rescued. Mrs. Thompson, who is in Poplar Elospital,and who had both legs fractured, one of which has been am- putated, is not dead, as was reported, but is going on as well as can he expected—as are all the other patients detained there.

On Wednesday afternoon an inqurst was opened as to the death of 33 of the victims. Format evidence oi identification haying been taken, the inquiry was ad- journed until Tuesday morning next.

W ABM NGTON FIELD CLUB.

This club visited Frodsham Marsh, under the 1 leadership of Dr. Billups, on Saturday afternoon. There was only a small attendance, presumably owing to the threatening appearance of the weather, but those whose robust faith impelled them to brave the elements met with an ample reward, for on arriving at Frodsham the rain ceased and several hours were both pleasantly and proti;ably employed in studying the fauna and flora or the marsh land and meadows which stretch down to the river and which are rarely visited by the ordinary excur- sionist. A number of plants occur here which are not found elsewhere in the district; the Zannichellia pedunculata, not hitherto recorded as occurring in Cheshire, was found growing in profusion at the west end of the marsh ; the interesting and curious frogbit and the bladder wort also occur in some of the ditches. The semi-aquatic hunting-spider (Pirata piratica) and the wolf-spider (monticola) were taken, and it was evident that under more favourable atmospheric conditions many other ar- achnidans would have been observed. Several re- cords were made amongst the beetles; the whirligig (Gurinus natator). so familiar to the school boy in- tent on the capture of the wily sticklebactc, was plentiful, but in addition a new local species decor- ated with handsome red longitudinal stripesais well as three species of Bombinium rewarded the patient search of two of the party.

===== 0002601_18980624_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
letter 6 to the fattitor.

A PARENT AND '`OBSERYSR'S" NOMS.

Sir.—Will you kindly allow me a small space hi your valuablu papsr to substantiate the "inspired correspon- dent"of Stockton Heath's statement with reference to the children attending the Church schools with their hair in plaits and also to open the eyes of blind" Observer " for is he a master in Israel and knows not these things ? If not I may tell him that a short time ago my children along with other children were called upon to take part in an entertainment for the benefit of the church or school funds. In consequence of this, I had to make a sacrifice through buying new clothing and other things necessary for the occasion, which Las a member of the lower strata-of society,could not so well afford and in addition to this the children were asked to put their hair in plaits,which they did,and also attended school in the same, others in curling pins and curl papers but nothing was said. Why? I would ask " Observer." It has since so happened that these same children were given a treat and an entertainment by the officials of their own Sunday School, when they were called upon to take their part in the entertainment. To prevent them from breaking a law passed by Parliament they were sent to school, with their hair in plaits, not curl papers. They were ordered at once to take their hair out of plait and to remain in that state during the school hours without being given a brush or comb, and also sent home in that state. What would the Government Inspector have recorded had he appeared on the scene ? again I would ask" Observer" For "Observer's" information I may say that I placed the matter in the hands of the Chairman for the then Parish Council, who afterwards informed me that the curate-in-charge was sorry for what had taken place and that it should not occur again. If Observer is not a married man I say he should get married and probably he may have a parental feeling, it not" inspiration." So without .taking up any more space I will leave the question to your many readers for their judgment as to whether the "inspired correspond- nt" was right or wrong.—Yours Stc., Stockton Heath.

J. H.

===== 0002601_18980624_art0137.txt | included=true | included =====
LOCAL CASE AT DEVONSHIRE ASSIZES.

DAMAGED CLAY AT RUNCORN.

At the Devonshire Assizes at Exeter on Wednesday, before the Lord Chief Justice of England, a case came on for hearing of some local interest. WHITENNY V: PLAYER.—Mr. Bodilly was counsel for the plaintiff, instructed by Messra. T. and J. Hutchings, and Mr. Duke was for defendants, instructed by Messrs. Holman, Birciawood and Co. This was an action for damages for breach of charter party and bill of lading, and for the ship Natela being unable to carry a certain cargo. Counsel for the plaintiff explaiaed that Messr3. Whitenny and Company were clay merchants of Teign- mouth. Devon. Oa the Bth February they contracted with the defendant to send a quantity of alumina clay— which was the most valuable Sound in Devonshire—per the Natala to Ruucorn. It was the defendant's duty to clean the schooner for the reception of the mg°. On arriving at Runcorn it was found that the whole of the clay had become impregnated with pieces of coal, which rendered it in an unmarketable condition. It wa3 also damp: Evidence was givm by Mr. George Henry Jarvis, ship- broker, Teignmouth, and Mr. Michael Riley, weighman in the employ of the Manchester Ship Canal at Runcorn and Mr. David Henry Dunning, agent at Runcorn for the plaintiff's forwarding agents. Tne last two witnesses both stated that they saw small pieces of coal in the clay. Mr. Dunning added that in consequence of his not re- ceiving a clean bill from the Canal Company he refused to pay the freight. The Captain came to him and ad- mitted that some coal did fall into the clay during the voyage, but that he believed they had taken it all out The wharf stockman in the employment of the Man- chester Ship Canal Cempany (A. Hays) produced a sample of the pieces of coal, which he picked out of the Clay when it was in a heap on Runcorn Quay. Mr. George Leeson, J.P„ managing director of the Minton, Limited, potters, of Scoke-on-Trent,who had 47 years' experieece, said that bits of cell and coal slack were found in the clay in lacient quantity to render it uselese for the best pottery work, aleheugh it might be asked for an inferior kind of pottery. The Lord Chief Justice : What difference in Tahiti per ton would the presenee of coal make ? Witness said the clay with the coal which he saw at Runcorn, the subject of the present action, was worth about 103. per ton,and if it had been free from coal it would have been worth 16i. or 17s. per ton. Cross-examined by Mr. Duke: In clay purchased from Minton's bits of coal sometimes appeared. He knew that lignite was commonly found in the clay in question,but that could be eliminated, whereas bits of coal, owing to its brittle nature and its readiness to break into fine par- ticles could not be. His Lordship: This gentleman reduces the damages considerably i.e., to 51. or 63. a ton. Mr. Duke : It is so, assuming that the whole parcel was damaged. His Lordship That would make the damages for the entire quantity £BO or £7O. Mr.Ed w ard Grestbutce .President of the North Stafford shire7Chamber of Commerce, and agent for the plaintiff for the sale of the clay in the Potteries, said that in con- sequence of the presence of the coal the value of the clay was reduced from 17s. a ton to 103. Cross-examined : The clay was offered at public auction at Ruacern, and witness, who was the only bidder, bought it at 103. a ton for the benefit of all parties concerned. Mr. Duke, for the defendant, said the ship hod a Stormy passaee, and a few bits of coal from a ledge -Olden had been overlooked in the cleaning fell into the- clay His client was prepared to pay what was reason. able damages. He considered that that could lee met by an allowance of is. per ton,on what would be an equi- valent of the cost of sifting the coal from the clay. For the defence Messrs. F. J. Mellen, Henry William and William McGill, employed on the schooner, deposed that the clay was only slightly impregaated with coal. Mr. Lucas, senior member of the firm of Messrs. Lucas and Son, clay merchants, of Berslem, stated that he con- sidered that the depreciation in the value of the clay by the permeation of the coal was only Is. per ton. If there had been more coal mixed with it the depreciation would have been mere. A verdict was given for the plaintiff's Company with £5O damages.

CYCLE ACCIDENT AT EARLESTOWN.

On Friday night an old man named John War- field, 81 years of age, of 11, Princes-street, E tries- town, was knocked down by a bicycle, and sus- tained a fractured thigh. A lad named Thomas Judge, 16 years ot age was riding a bicycle dowa Earle-street, when be saw the old man crossing the street in the direction of the Viaduct Hotel. He shouted and the old man hesitated as if uncertain which way to go, and as Judge was too close to avoid him, he ran into him and knocked him over. Warfield was removed to Warrington Infirmary, where he at present remains, and is progressing satisfactorily. HOPE OF EARLESTJWN LODGE. 1.0.G.T. nc-Nic FRox TIIN VULCAN.

On Saturday- the tnachinemen and turners em- ployed at the Vulcan Foundry had their annual pic nic, the destination this year being Hawarden. Leaving Newton-le-Willows by the 16.9 a.m, train in reserved carriage,the party numbering 20 reached Chester about eleven o'clock, where they were met at the Albion Hotel by a wagonette and proceeded on their journey to Hawarden. On the way they called at Broughton Hall, where the party obtained some of the fine roses grown there. The Glynne Arms, Hawarden was reached about 1.15, where lunch was partaken of. Afterwards the party strolled through the Castle grounds, the park keeper explaining the most interesting points, chiefly the old Castle which is said to be 700 years old, and from the top of which a view of Eaton Hall and New Brighton Tower could be obtained. Leaving the grounds dinner was partaken of at the Glynn° Arms, after which a visit was paid to Howarden Church in order to see the new stained glass window which has been recently fixed, in memory of the deceased Statesman. The return from Hawarden was made about 6 o'clock, a halt being made at the City Arras, Sultney, where some harmony was indulged in, and Chester was reached about 7.15, and the remainder of the time was spent at the Buwling Green Hotel, where in addition to a game of bowls, songs were given by Messrs. It. Smith, H. Williams, J. Smith, J. Sweetlove, W. Blower, J. Jones and S. Hyde. A hearty vote ot thanks was accorded Mr. R. Smith and Mr. J. Boyer, on the motion of Mr. J. Singleton, seconded by Mr. E. Jones for the excellent arrang,3ments made. Chester was lett at 8.35 and Earlestown LeaChed about 10 o'clock after a most enjoyable outing.

JUMBLE SALE AT ASTON-BY-SUTTON.

On Tuesday afternoon, a most successful jumble sale was held in Aston Hall, (kindly lent by Squire Talbot for the occasion) in aid of the church at Aston. Although the weather was anything but favourable a good num- ber of people attended the sale, and the large ronm,when the goods were set out presented quite a busy appear- ance.liirs.Wray, Halton,declared ti s sale open, and said she thought it was quite wonderful, the improvements that had taken place during the last few years, and if the sale was the SUCCeBO they all hoped it would be the church was to be still further beautified. There was one thing in Aston to which she thought as a parish they had much cause to be thankful for, and that was the deep interest that the Squire and his wife took in parochial matters. She always thought it a pity that the laity did not realism more fully how much the suc- cess of a parish generally depended upon them ; for a clergyman, however earnest and hardvrcrking he might be, could not accomplish very much alone. The following ladies rendered valuable assistance in furthering the work of the sale : Mesdames Talbot, Dunn, Bate, Stormont, and Carruthers, and the Misses Linaker (2), Bate, Davies. Osborne,and Talbot.—Alraost the whole of the goods cffered were diposed of and a substantial sum realised towards the funds for deco- rating and cleaning the walls of the church.

THE EXAMINEE. FRIDAY, TUNE 24, 148.

SHOCKING FA.TALITY AT TIAYDOCK.

A PITBROW GIRL KILLED..

Elizabeth R)wley. aged twenty-three, a single woman, residing at Cipper House row. Blackbrook, Haydock, was shockingly injured and killed on Saturday at the Princess Pit, Haydock,one of Messrs. R. Evans and Co.'s collieries. Deceased was a pitbrow girl, and worked in connection with the coalscreens. S.aortly after eleven o'clock she came from under the screens and walk.ld out on to the main line cf the colliery railway. She unfor- tunately, failed to notice five v:aggnas that were coming along the line, by which she was knocked down and killed on the spot. Mr. F. A. Jones, deputy-coroner, on Tuesday held an inquest at the Waggon Hotel, Ilavdock, on the body of Elizabeth Row ley,a coal waggon trimmer at the Princess Pit, Haydock, belonging to Messrs. Richard Evans and Co., who was killed at the colliery on Saturday morn- ing. Mr. Hall (her Majesty's inspector of mines), Mr. John Robinson (colliery manager),and Mr. Latham (Sur- face manager) were present at the inquiry. James Macdonald, fourteen years of age, of Ashton-in- , Makerfield, lain that abut a quarter-past ten on Satur- day morning last he was on the ji..ger, when he saw deceased leave the screens. Sue walkel on up the middle of the main line for about thirty-six yards. He saw the engine behind her with nine empty wa.,g.rons, and called out" Elizabeth "once. She took no notice. He then saw the waggon knock her down. When he first saw her the waggons were forty yards away.

In reply to Mr. Hall, witness said the girls very often went for" pop." As the engine went by him it was no; whistling. The waggons were going pretty fast. He tried to attract the attention of the deceased, but not that of the driver, as he could not. Thomas Pimblett, braliesman, of Chinch-road, flay- dock, said he was between the first and second waggons on the buff-Ts on the left side. He was looking in front of him. There was about ten yards in front of him that he could not see. He did not see the deceased until he felt the waggons shake, and then he saw her beneath him with her head on the outside the line and her legs on the inside. She was dead. He whistled to the delver to stop. About five waggensand the first pair of wheels of the sixth waggon had passed over her. He thought deceased must have crossed in front of the waggons, be- cause he did not see her before. James Smith, engine-driver, said be was in charge of the train on Saturday, He received the signal from the brakesman Pimblett, and proceeded down the line a: about eight or nine miles an hour. He was looking o t over the left side of the engine, and the fireman over the right. He did not know why the brakesman, who was on this second waggon, could no; see the girl in front, particularly if she had proceeded for thirty-six yards, according to the lad Macdonald's statement. Mr. Hall said he had visited the scene of the accident, and if they had been looking out there would have been no difficulty in seeing the deceased. He did not think that the brakesman had been looking out, as he ought to have been. The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death,"and presented that they desired to urge the importance of kesm en keeping a good lookout.

BURTONWOOE SEWERAGE.

- The Suavayba reported that all openings of any hind by which sewage could get to Brook-lane had been fill3d in and that the new pumps were fixed and in working order. 13v actual experiment he had ascertained what amount of sewage was running from the village. At five o'clock p.m.,en Wednesday the 5:6 May the sewage was turned into an empty tank.lt ran until 8-25 p.m.on Sunday the 29th May before filling the tank,which holds 17.325 gallons. The sewage had therefore run at the rate of 173 gallons per hour and as there are 163 houses in cornrction with the sewers the amount from each house was 251 gallons per day of 24 hours.

CYCLING.

CycHats are apt to run from one extreme to the other in matters of luorication ; they overdo it and swill the bea-ings with oil wrier) the fit is on, and aZ other time 3 forget all about it and let them ran dry. The latest im- provement should prevent this. It is a lubrication indi- cator which show:, at once when the bearing requires oil. It consists of a small metallic cylinder fixed to the hub in the same way as an ordinary lubricator, but provided with a small hole covered with glass or some such trans- parent material through which the oil is visible,enabling one to ascertain in a moment the amount of lubricant in the cylinder, and So arranged that should the oil riot reach the ball race, it will no; be visible in the indi- cator.

=Tiane are timls when it i 4 —very necessary that the handle-bar should be able to stand a fair amount of twist- in.: strain without the pillar turning in its socket. It may happen chat you inadvertently get your wheels in a ,rut, and a sudden twist of the steering is imperative to lift it out, unless you want to try conclusions with the wagon coining M the opposite direction; or you may be travelling at a fair rate downhill when your front wheel catches astonc., and in the effort to pu:l it straig'at the pillar lzvists in tbe sockets, and over you COMB.

Ai amusing case was heard at the Southwark County Court. the other day. Plaintiff an.l defendant were snop• mates. A few wePks ago the plaintiff announced to de- fenifant his intention of looking, out for a " bike " as he was a " bit queer in health " inci thought a little riding would recruit it. Defendant said he had a " beauty " for sale and eventually plaintiff saw it wad agreed to purchase it for £5.

Plaintiff explained to defealtut Lhat he was "quite a stranger among machines" and that he did not know any more about them than the "man in the moon." He also expressed a hops that defendant, being a shopmate, OUidiflOt "do him one "over the transaction. Dofen- dant replied that he could not think of such a thing, but would rather " do" a stranger—and the machine was in " thorough good working order, and would, with care last two years." Plaintiff said the defendant had promised to teach him to ride but with the exception of two occa- sions, he was always cut when he went for his lessons.

After riding two or three tires the tyres " busted." A bicycle repairer on hearing what he paid for it laughed at him and told him it was not worth a sovereign. Re further said he would be unable to repair it. After a lot of "humming and hawing "defendants son mended it. Two days after it went wrong, but defendant refused to have anything more to do with the thing.

The defendant in the course of a long statement volunteered that the plaintiff had been running the machine without any " hair " in the tyres, and conse- quently the rim had cut them He (defendant) promised to show him how to pump them, and also bow to use the spanner (this with emphasis) "if necessary." That was more than anysne else would have done when selling a second-hand machine. Judgment was given for defend- ant. His Honour- said he could not help peopls who made improvithnt bargains with their eyes wide open.

He could speak of art instance in Warrington when one of their members re- ceived from the Board £4O grant with which he bought an organ. The accident was caused by blindness. Bro. I). A SCENE AT WEST-ON.

CHARGE OF ASSAULT AGAINST THE

POLICE.

A VIOLENT PRISONER.

On Monday, at the Police Court, Runcorn, before Messrs. E. Sakman and A. B. Norman, county magis- trat,s. Harry Chailes Melyneux, was charged on bail with using obscene and profane language at Weston on the 16th t f June,and further with assaulting P.C. Morris at the same time and place, whilst in the execution of his duty.—There was ake a counter-summons for assault taken out against P.C. Morris.—Both defendants pleaded not guilty. Mr. Joseph Burton, solicitor, Runcorn, appeared for Molyneux. and Mr. Briggs, Sc.ockport, appeared for the police.—The deputy -chief constable of the county was also present. Mr. Briggs opened the cases, and said he would ask their worohips indulgence as he intended laying before them the whole facts of the case. It app.ated that cn the 16th June—(last Thursday )—about 6.30 the defen- dant Molyneux ss his wife playing with a neighbour. For some reason known best to himself—

Mr. Burton : I objnt ; it has nothing to do with the cap.

Mr. Briggs : lam not going to say what this man did.

Mr. Burton : It is prejudicial to the case. Moly- neux,neither kicked.struck,or did anything to the officer he only tried to prevent the cfficer hurting him. Guy Pierce, Heath-road, Weston, said he was in his house and heard a noise and en going out saw both the ' men struggling for Morris's stick. Morris had hold of defendant by the throat,and his stick was in the other hand. They strugg,ed for some time and fell. Morris then struck Molyneux with his fist in the ribs. Witness saw Morris try to kick Mc-Apneas. It was a solid blow over the head on to the shoulder that Morris afterwards administered with the stick. D4endant (Molynoux) said " let me go and I will go quiet," but the officer woull not let him go. Cross-examined • He was in the house in the front room upsr airs and did not see the struggle commence. Molyneux never struck back• at all.. He had not spoken to Molyneux since.

William Norton said he saw the officer kick the prisoner twice. The officer was on his feet at the time. The first time he missed him, and the second time he kicked bim in the stomach. Witness said" You are

wrong to abuse the man " and advised Molyneux to go quietly. Molyneux replied "He woe't let me." He believed that the prisoner would have gone quietly if he had been lett to do so, Mlyneux never struck the policeman at all, and behaved qdite quietly. He saw the officer with the strap of ths staff in his hand, but did not know how it got there. lie saw all that took pla:e but never saw the prisoner with the staff at all.

Cross-examined by M. Brigs : Tha men were in the entry when he saw them, and there was big crowd of people there. He had been Hi years in Weston and knew Molyneux very well, but had never been in the man's company at all save meeting him occasionally in the streets. Molyneux nevet attempted to kick back or retaliate in any way. Prisoior did put his band into the officer's pockets and take hold of the s.aff, but never drew it.

By Mr. Burton: The officer had hold of the staff and pulled it out of his pocket himself, but Molyneux also had hold of it.

Mr. Burton said that was his case. True it was that the police had to be protected whilst in the execution of their duty, but they are not to allow themselves to so far lose their temper as to assault people. If the officer had never met the man he would have gone home and there would have been no bother. From the evidence of the witnesses it was evident that from first to last he had never struck the officer at all; and he took it that the offence was committed by the officer himself. With regard to the using uf obscene language, they could see that the prisoner had not made use of anything beyond what he admitted. Everyone of the witnesses were in. dependent and agreed in their evidence, whilst the officer called no one. lie would therefore ask the tench to decide that his client did not commit the offence alleged. Mr. Briggs characterised the whole evidence as un- sound and trumpery, and pointed out several discrep- ancies in the evidence adduced. There was a man who ,en his own evidence had cursed his wife, and caught I her by the hair, and pulled her about; ten leaving her undoubtedly in a passion he went out and assaulted the policeman so it was not so difficult to credit the police- man's story. Then again, with regard to the disagree• ment of the witnesses, one swore that the police officer hit the defendant with a stick and the other than it was with his fists that the offence was committed. Finally he said that it was absurd to conceive that the policeman would probe and kick a man who could give no reason for his doing so, whilst at the same time it was lett to : one of his witnesses to suggest a reasen,and that reason was nothing but" be did it for spite." To the young officer a charge of this kind meant something very serious indeed,if it was proved it meant his professional ruin, so he felt confident that the magistrates would weigh the evidence,and not be induced to put too much credence in the evidence of a number of witnesses who had evidently combined together. They (the magistrates) would know from experience how inuch weight to attach to the oaths of people that were interested parties and he felt certain that the young officer would be cleared. The Bench after a short retirement returned into court, and the chairman said "Harry Charles Molyneux, we fiud you guilty of using obscene lauguage,and impose a fine of 51 and Bi. 61. Costs, or seven days for that offence. —Yoe will also be fined £1 and costs (£2 3s 61) for the assault upon Police-caastable Morris, or one months' imprisonment in default. The sentences run consecutively.-0a the application of dlr. Brigg's advo- cates fee was alto wed.— Die charge against M wris was dismissed.

WARRINGTON v. MANCHESTER OSBORNE.

. These teams played a friendly match on Tuesday evening at the Corporation Baths, Warrington, before a good number of spectators. Manchester Osborne were the champions of England for 1894-5-6-7. The following were the teams,Warrington: A Robertson goal;S Beaman and Boscow, backs; Furness, half back; W Ashton, A. Ashton, and Hughes, forwards. Manchester Osborne : G Hamblett, goal; E Robinson and J W Shaw, backs ; Prince half back; J U Derbyshire, J Robinson and Sutton, forwards. Prince and Sutton played as substitutes for Osborne. The homesters also were short of two or three of the first team men. War- rington took the shallow end and were first to secure the ball. W. Ashton scored the first goal. Good passing resulted in Derbyshire scoring for the visitors. After some lively work Hughes essayed a shot for goal but was unfortunate. Pressing, tho 03borne team took the ball up to the Warrington end and Derbyshire shot for goal bat the Warrington custodian proved himself equal to the occasion. A little before half time A. Ashton,who was covering Derbyshire placed another goal to the credit of Warrington. On resumption of play, the locals taking the deep end, a slight alteration was made in the disposition of the Warrington team. W. Ashton was placed to cover D,srbyshiro in plue of A. Ashton. Securing the ball by splendid passing, the visitors tock ' it well up, and J. Robinson shot a goal. Derbyshire, backed up by the splendid passing combination of Osborne, now began to make Chinas lively for the War- rington custodian, giving tits ilsaty to do. ' Warrington played well out were forced to play on the defensive. Two of tho visitors forwards rushed the ball to the Warrington posts but failed to score through Illbertson'a smartness. Taking advantage of every opportunity to shoot,though his attempts were repeatedly baulked, Derbyshire again scored. Sutton shortly followed this up by a long shot for goal which scored for Osborne. The Warrington team both individually and collectively played very well but as expected Osborne showed themselves much su- perior by their passing and generally finished style. FRODSHAII.

INQUEST.—On Wednesday morning an inquest was held at Frodsham on the body of a labourer named Thomas Ashbrook, aged 63,residing at the Quay. From the evidence it appeared that the deceased hung himself either on Monday morning or early on the Sunday night, as when the body was discovered it appeared as if death had occurred some time previously. lie was last seen alive on the Sunday morning. For some time past he had been drinking heayily,and probably committed his rash act whilst suffering from mental derangement. The jury returned a verdict in accordance with the evid- ence.

PRESENTATI .—Mr. J. Lockett, senior clerk in the goods department at Frodsham Railway station,has been presented with a testimonial on the occasion of his mar- riage, he having been clerk at the station for the past 22 years. The presentation took place in the commercial- room of the Bear's Paw Hotel, and was made by Mr. Lambert, stationmaster, who spoke of the sterling merit of Mr. Lockett in his official capacity, and in handing over the various articles, consisting of a dinner and tea set, sabscribed for by the Railway employee ; and a silver mounted biscuit barrel from Messrs. Joseph Ash- worth and Sons, Frodsham Bridge ; and a purse of gold from the traders using the station ; said that it was the earnest wish of all the subscribers that Mr. Lockett would enjoy many years of happy married life.—Mr. T. Potts, late stationmaster of Frodsham, also endorsed all the previous speaker had said, as he could speak with emphasis, having had 7 years' experience of Mr. Lockett's worth. Mr. Downes Massie (of ttre firm of Messrs. Heywood and Massie. Weaver Cnemicsl Works Frodsham Bridge), and Mr. William Youd, also spoke in felicitous terms of Mr. Lockett, who responded with marked feelings of emotion. PAROCHIAL COMMITTEE.

LATEST NEWS. THE WAR. THE AMERICAN TROOPS LANDED IN CUBA. MOLE Sr. NICHOLAS, THIIRSDAY. Advices have been received here from Playadelestes• near Santiago, that 5,000 of General Shifter's trocpi were safely landed thereat two o'clock yesterday. Ther actually disembarked at Messrs. Carnegie's wharf,which before the war was used for the shipment of the iron-ore' which Messrs. Caraegie i:nported from mines some miles inland. By midnight ad the troops were landed. TH.E CAPTURE OF CA.VITE. BARCELOMA, THURSDAY. The journal" Guerra" publishes a detailed acoount of ' events in Manila. According to the correspondent, the British Consul has been displaying great partiality tO* , wards Americans. When the Spaniards abandoned Cavite it is added the Americans allowed the populace to pillage the town, and the natives looteil the place SO thoroughly that they even took the clothes from the ; backs of the Spanish residents. ATTACK ON AN ENGLISH CONSULATE. The Cadiz correspondent of the " New York Herald telegraphs that a mob of ieactionaries made a violent' attack en the English Consulate there, but were dile' persed by the police. Mr. Schreiner's resolution expressing want of confid- ence in the Ministry of Sir Gordon Sorigg has bees carried in the House of Assembly by 41 votes to 36.
